### F-EW-1215 — "Several of the Carmelites were mistaken about her"
- Claim: The Articles admit that several Carmelites were mistaken about her, and one asked, after her death, what there was to say about her.

- Source: D3 art. 107 p. 118 (PDF 132)

### F-EW-1216 — Walking for a missionary
- Claim: Ordered by the infirmarian to walk a quarter of an hour a day in the garden, she walked even when exhausted; to a Sister who told her to rest, she said she offered each step for some tired missionary far away.

### F-EW-1217 — Made to stand though feverish
- Claim: During her illness the community sang a hymn at the Sacred Heart hermitage in the garden; she arrived exhausted and sat; a religious signed to her to stand; she stood to the end in spite of fever and breathlessness.

### F-EW-1218 — The kitchen remark: "never done anything worth speaking about"
- Claim: Resting in her cell during recreation, she overheard a Sister in the kitchen say that the prioress would be puzzled what to write in her obituary, since, "amiable as she is", she had "certainly never done anything worth speaking about". The infirmarian heard it too; Thérèse said she had long been indifferent to the opinion of creatures.
- When / where: summer 1897, her cell next to the kitchen
- People: unnamed kitchen Sister; the infirmarian (Sr Saint-Stanislas, per other sources — not named here)
- Witness: Prioress's Epilogue (Mère Agnès)
- Source: D6 Epilogue p. 202 (PDF 236)

- Confidence: high

### F-EW-1219 — Painting on demand: an ugly picture for a difficult Sister
- Claim: Sisters brought objects to her to decorate as gifts for the prioress's feast; one found fault instead of thanking her. She painted something "ridiculous and not in good taste" to please a Sister who often mortified her, and her last painting, months before death, was for that Sister.

- Claim: Mère Agnès testified that a Sister asked the feverish Thérèse to paint for her; Agnès objected; the nun insisted; Thérèse's face flushed from the inner struggle; that evening she wrote to Agnès that she was glad Agnès had seen her imperfection.

### F-EW-1221 — A blanket folded in four: 500 pin-point burns
- Claim: Céline testified: after the cautery needle was applied — she once counted 500 applications — Thérèse went at night to her straw mattress; Céline, assistant infirmarian without permission to give her a mattress, folded a large blanket in four under her sheet.

### F-EW-1222 — Father Corbinelli and little Aloysius: Mère Hermance
- Claim: During refectory reading of the Life of St Aloysius, old Mère Hermance du Cœur de Jésus called Thérèse "little Aloysius" and herself "old Father Corbinelli"; near death Thérèse sent word that she had seen Corbinelli's grave close to Aloysius's; Hermance took it as a prophecy; she died one year later and the two graves lie side by side.

### F-EW-1223 — Pauline warned Thérèse the manuscript might be burned
- Claim: Pauline told Thérèse that the manuscript could be used for publication, and also that the prioress might burn it. Thérèse answered that it would not matter; God would use other means.
- When / where: summer 1897, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès; Mère Marie de Gonzague (the "notre mère" meant)
- Witness: Mère Agnès; HEARD
- Source: PO 1, f. 158v, Session 7, 16 Aug 1910; again f. 239r, Session 18, 1 Sept 1910
- Original words: « Oh! mais qu'est-ce que cela ferait; c'est que le bon Dieu ne voudrait pas se servir de ce moyen »
- Translation: "Oh, but what would that matter? It would mean God did not want to use this means."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high — told twice, consistent
- Notes: Pauline states it was never the custom at Lisieux for nuns to write autobiographical notes for their circulars (f. 158v).

### F-EW-1224 — Pauline's account of how Histoire d'une âme was published
- Claim: Pauline says she proposed the publication. Gonzague sent Pauline's copy to Fr Godefroy Madelaine, Premonstratensian prior of Mondaye. On his report Bishop Hugonin gave the imprimatur on 7 March 1898. The copy was printed after some retouches Fr Godefroy indicated. Pauline gives October 1898 as the publication date.
- When / where: 1897–1898, Lisieux / Mondaye / Bayeux
- People: Mère Agnès; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Fr Godefroy Madelaine; Bishop Flavien Hugonin
- Witness: Mère Agnès; first-hand

- Extraction ID: A-F10
### F-EW-1225 — Treatments in 1897
- Claim: At the end of Lent 1897 her state worsened; the fever became continuous. She was treated with blistering plasters (vésicatoires), pointes de feu (cauterizing needles), tincture of iodine and rubbings. All failed. For some weeks earlier, because of a persistent cough, she had been put on a meat diet.
- When / where: spring 1897, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Dr de Cornière
- Witness: Mère Agnès; SAW
- Source: PO 1, f. 241v–242r, Session 18, 1 Sept 1910
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —

### F-EW-1226 — Dr de Cornière: "don't wish to keep her in this state"
- Claim: The monastery doctor, Dr de Cornière, said it was dreadful how she suffered and that they should not wish to keep her alive in that state. He was amazed at her unchanging patience and her smile.
- When / where: summer 1897, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Dr Alexandre-Damase de Cornière; Thérèse
- Witness: Mère Agnès; HEARD
- Source: PO 1, f. 242r, Session 18, 1 Sept 1910
- Original words: « C'est affreux ce qu'elle souffre, ne désirez pas la conserver dans cet état. »

### F-EW-1227 — The meat-juice scene: "not even a good nun"
- Claim: Opposition: At the worst of her illness a lay sister offered her meat juice. Thérèse gently refused, saying it would make her vomit, and humbly asked pardon. The lay sister, unedified, told another sister that Thérèse was not a saint, not even a good religious. Thérèse heard of it and was so happy she confided it to a sister, who later told Pauline it was her most edifying memory of Thérèse.
- When / where: summer 1897, infirmary
- People: Thérèse; an unnamed lay sister; another unnamed sister
- Witness: Mère Agnès; partly SAW, partly heard from the confidante
- Source: PO 1, f. 232r, Session 17, 31 Aug 1910
- Original words: « Sœur Thérèse de l'Enfant Jésus non seulement n'est pas une sainte, mais elle n'est pas même une bonne religieuse. »
- Translation: "Sr Thérèse of the Child Jesus is not only not a saint, she's not even a good nun."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — Pauline does not recall whether Thérèse drank it in the end
- Notes: A critical opinion from inside the community.
- Extraction ID: A-F68
### F-EW-1228 — Dissenter 2: "what will our mother write in her circular?"
- Claim: Opposition: A lay sister said during Thérèse's illness: she wondered what the prioress could write in Thérèse's circular; Thérèse had been pampered and had not gained virtue through struggle like the rest; she was sweet and good, but it was natural. Thérèse overheard it and told Pauline. This lay sister, now dead, at other times said Thérèse was a saint.
- When / where: summer 1897 (DE 29 July), Lisieux Carmel
- People: an unnamed lay sister (dead by 1910); Thérèse; Mère Agnès
- Witness: Mère Agnès; heard from Thérèse, who HEARD it
- Source: PO 1, f. 241r–v, Session 18, 1 Sept 1910; variant NV f. 267v (29 July)

- Confidence: high
- Notes: The NV version (29 July) is a remark "made at recreation" and reported by a sister: she practised virtue, but not virtue acquired by humiliations and suffering. Thérèse's reply: "And I who have suffered so much since my earliest childhood!"

- Claim: She said she liked St Aloysius Gonzaga less than Théophane Vénard, because Aloysius's Life says he was sad even at recreation, whereas Vénard was always cheerful.

- Claim: Pauline says you could tell Thérèse was suffering when she was gayer at recreation and quicker at work. Asked why she was so joyful, she said: because she was in pain; nothing gave her joy like pain.

- Claim: After her death almost all new postulants came because of her, the first being Mère Marie-Ange de l'Enfant-Jésus (a Breton, prioress, who brought the Cause to the Church, offered her life for it and died at 28). - Claim: Distinctive: Pauline says her life was very simple, otherwise she could not be the model of "little souls", which she called "her way". - Claim: Sisters wanted to kill the flies that kept bothering her. She always begged them to spare them: they were her only enemies, and God said to forgive one's enemies.

### F-EW-1236 — The jealous sister who ordered odd paintings; Thérèse's last use of her brushes
- Claim: A sister who was very jealous of her and never missed a chance to mortify her asked her to paint decorations on work she made for the prioress's feast. This sister was very eccentric and asked for bizarre subjects in poor taste. Thérèse never refused; she looked for models and followed her odd instructions. In 1897 she again painted small works for this sister — the last time she used her brushes. Pauline notes the sister has since left the Order.

### F-EW-1237 — Prayers for the dead to the last; the "heroic vow" known only second-hand
- Claim: Even in great pain she said six Our Fathers and Hail Marys every evening for the souls in purgatory; she said the devil tried to make her forget them but rarely succeeded. She begged to keep saying the Office of the Dead for deceased sisters until she was totally exhausted. Pauline knows from Sr Geneviève that she made the "heroic vow" for the souls in purgatory; perhaps Thérèse told Pauline too, but Pauline does not remember surely.
- When / where: 1897
- People: Thérèse; Sr Geneviève
- Witness: Mère Agnès; the vow HEARD from Sr Geneviève
- Source: PA 6, [441], S15 9 Jul 1915
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for prayers; medium for the vow (hearsay, she says so)
- Notes: —

### F-EW-1238 — Pauline wiped and kept her tears on fine linen
- Claim: When Thérèse cried from gratitude, Pauline felt urged to collect the tears. Whenever she could, she wiped them with a fine cloth, which became soaked. Thérèse let her do it, out of kindness to her. Later Pauline adds that Thérèse knew it was not to dry her face but to keep them as a revered souvenir.
- When / where: summer 1897, infirmary
- People: Mère Agnès; Thérèse
- Witness: Mère Agnès about herself
- Source: PA 6, [454], S16 12 Jul 1915; [496], S19 15 Jul 1915

- Claim: Asked whether others received her confidences about her future mission, Pauline said Thérèse opened up like this only in strict intimacy with her sisters, and above all with Pauline; perhaps a little with Sr Marie de la Trinité. She was convinced Thérèse meant these words seriously and never joked on such subjects.

- People: Thérèse; her sisters; Sr Marie de la Trinité

### F-EW-1240 — Publish at once, tell no one but Mother; if it is burned, no matter
- Claim: On her deathbed she said with assurance the manuscript must be published without delay after her death. If Pauline waited or spoke of it to anyone except Mother, the devil would set a thousand traps. As with Joan of Arc, God's will would be done despite men's jealousy. It would help all kinds of souls except those in extraordinary ways. Asked what if Mother threw it in the fire, she said she would not feel the least pain or doubt about her mission; God would answer her another way.
- When / where: summer 1897, infirmary
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès; Mère Marie de Gonzague ("Notre Mère")
- Witness: Mère Agnès, HEARD
- Source: PA 6, [502]–[503], S19 15 Jul 1915

- Notes: The fire question points to fear of Gonzague's reaction.

- Claim: Pauline says the nuns esteemed her more than any other, but during her life did not imagine a beatification. Pauline herself, who saw her as a saint especially in her last illness, did not think of canonization, believing one needed miracles and striking deeds in life.

- Claim: At the end of her life Pauline heard her say she had never done her own will on earth, and so God would do all her wills in heaven.

- Claim: After Thérèse's death some nuns had extraordinary experiences such as perfumes, which Pauline calls "secondary and hard to prove". ### F-EW-1244 — With Pauline she only listened, never confided
- Claim: When the prioress let Mère Agnès go and speak to Thérèse sometimes, Thérèse had no matching permission to open her own heart. So she only listened to her "little mother" and told nothing of her own thoughts. She could have obtained permission with one word, but said one must not seek permissions that soften the martyrdom of religious life.

- People: Mère Agnès; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Thérèse

- Claim: Almost all the nuns smelled mysterious perfumes at various times; Marie herself did four or five times, always when not thinking of it. She says she attaches less importance to these than to an inner grace.

### F-EW-1247 — Printing the manuscript: Mondaye review, circular, Gonzague's signature
- Claim: After Thérèse's death the manuscript was reviewed by Premonstratensian fathers of Mondaye (Fr Godefroy Madelaine, prior, and Fr Norbert), who urged publication and got the bishop's imprimatur. Agnès's aim was to send it to the Order's monasteries instead of the usual obituary circular. ### F-EW-1249 — "Two in a hundred" and the skeleton on holy pictures
- Claim: Hearing the doctor say that of a hundred people with her illness not more than two survived, she said pleasantly how unfortunate it would be if she were one of the two. To a sister who asked if she feared death, she said it frightened her when shown on pictures as a spectre, but that is not what death is; the catechism says it is the separation of soul and body.

### F-EW-1250 — Buried under blankets by the deaf old infirmarian
- Claim: Told to say by obedience what would relieve her, Thérèse, burning with fever, asked the first infirmarian to take off a blanket. The very old, somewhat deaf sister understood she was cold and piled on every blanket she could find, up over her head. Céline came back to find her streaming with sweat. Thérèse said she accepted all out of obedience, and since she smiled at each, the sister kept bringing more.

### F-EW-1251 — Useless remedies offered for a sick missionary; the "heroic act"
- Claim: In her illness she let herself be given the most repugnant and repeated remedies, knowing they were useless. She told Céline she offered these painful and useless treatments for an abandoned sick missionary who lacked care. She had made the "heroic act", giving the Virgin all her daily merits and all suffrages after her death for the souls in purgatory.

- Claim: Having suffered from lack of communion, she predicted they would not lack "our daily bread" after her death. Céline says that in 13 years there was only one day without it, for lack of a priest. Gonzague suddenly changed her practice and the chaplains were free to give daily communion.

- Claim: She had a statuette of her guardian angel in her girlhood room. Her patrons: St Martin, St Francis de Sales, St Teresa; she loved St John of the Cross's writings. - Claim: One evening in her illness the community went to the oratory of the Sacred Heart to sing a hymn. She followed with difficulty and had to sit during the singing. A sister called her to come and sing; she rose at once and joined the choir. - Claim: Neither Thérèse nor her sisters thought the memories would be published; they were family notes. - Claim: In her illness she pointed out to Céline that the first infirmarian always took very soft cloths to ease her a little: "we must take the same care of souls". Why do we not relieve souls with the same delicacy as bodies? All bodily penances, she said, were nothing next to charity.

- Claim: In her last illness she was glad to live in a cell where no one heard her cough. In the infirmary she would not let anyone sit up with her at night. She would not let the flies that tormented her be killed, saying they were her only enemies and she forgave them to obey Our Lord.

### F-EW-1283 — A sticky spoon and a vile remedy tasted drop by drop
- Claim: At dinner, if the handle of her knife or spoon was badly wiped and sticky, she kept it so, though this cost her greatly. In her illness Céline saw her drink a foul remedy drop by drop and urged her to gulp it; she said she tasted it on purpose, since she was forbidden big mortifications.

### F-EW-1284 — The foot-warmer, the painful dress, iced water and grapes
- Claim: In winter, when it was a little less cold, she smothered the foot-warmer given on the doctor's orders. She took her clothes as given; wearing a dress that hurt her badly, she said it was as indifferent to her "as to a Chinese man". In the refectory she ate all leftovers, as a poor person. In her last illness she did not ask for iced water or grapes, as merely pleasant. She was glad to have no copies of her poems, which she gave away as soon as written, though she would have liked copies to sing while working.
- When / where: Carmel; infirmary 1897
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: Céline
- Source: PA 8, [754], Session 35

- Claim: In her illness the sisters said perhaps at her death they would have a heavenly vision to console them. She replied quickly: oh no, she had never wanted extraordinary graces for herself; that was not her little way; she recalled her poem on Mary's life at Nazareth without raptures or miracles.

- People: Thérèse; her sisters
- Witness: Céline, present

- Claim: During her illness she painfully followed the community to the hermitage of the Sacred Heart and sat during the hymn. A sister signed to her to join the choir. Exhausted and unable to stand, she rose at once. When Céline reproached her afterward, she said she had got into the habit of obeying each sister as if God were showing His will.

### F-EW-1287 — The deaf old infirmarian and the extra blankets
- Claim: Burning with fever, she asked the first infirmarian to take a blanket off. The infirmarian, very old and a little deaf, understood she was cold and covered her right over her head. Céline came back to find her streaming with sweat. Smiling, Thérèse told the story without a word of complaint; she had accepted it joyfully in obedience, and the sister, seeing this, kept bringing more blankets, thinking to please her.

- People: Thérèse; the first infirmarian (Sr Saint-Stanislas, not named); Céline

### F-EW-1294 — Opposition turned: hostile sisters became devotees; Gonzague "amended"
- Claim: Opposition: After her death, sisters who had been hostile changed to veneration. None was keener to keep her souvenirs and portraits than Sr Saint-Vincent de Paul, the lay sister who had made her suffer; she went to every sister in turn to tell the bouquet story. Another sister composed a prayer to invoke her daily. Mother Marie de Gonzague "amended herself noticeably"; she received a signal grace before a portrait of Thérèse as a child and could not look at it without weeping. Through tears she told Céline: I alone can know what I owe her; oh, what she told me... all she reproached me with!... but so gently!
- When / where: Carmel, 1897–1904 (Gonzague died 1904)
- People: Sr Saint-Vincent de Paul; an unnamed sister; Mother Marie de Gonzague; Céline

### F-EW-1309 — Prediction that Abbé Hodierne would become chaplain and give daily communion
- Claim: Thérèse told Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur that a time would come when Abbé Hodierne would be chaplain and would give daily communion. Asked why she thought so, she said she hoped he would come and "we will be very happy with him." At that time the chaplain, Abbé Youf, was in good health. Hodierne was named chaplain on 15 Oct 1897. His first sermon took the text "Come and eat my bread" and invited daily communion, unasked.
- When / where: undated (some years before 1897), Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (Marie Martin); Abbé Hodierne; Abbé Youf (chaplain)

- Original words: « il viendra un temps où nous aurons peut-être pour aumônier monsieur l'abbé Hodierne, et lui, il nous donnera la communion tous les jours »
- Translation: A time will come when we will perhaps have Abbé Hodierne as chaplain, and he will give us communion every day.
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — second-hand, but TSA says she heard it before the fulfilment
- Notes: MdA (PO-14 [603r]) confirms a prediction of daily communion without naming Hodierne. TSA writes that Youf's death came "several years after" the words were spoken; Youf in fact died in Oct 1897.

- Claim: Opposition: Fearing sharper pain for her, TSA said she would pray God to soften it. Thérèse answered quickly: "No, no, one must let Him act." In 1915 TSA sets this after saying that in the last months the Mother Prioress "thought she ought to refuse to use the means of relieving her."

### F-EW-1315 — Deathbed joy at being called "not even a good religious"
- Claim: Opposition: Visiting Thérèse in her last illness, TSA found her radiant. Thérèse explained: a sister had visited and said, "If you knew how little you are loved and appreciated!" A few days earlier she had also heard a sister tell another: "I don't know why people talk so much about Sr Thérèse; she does nothing remarkable; we don't see her practise virtue; one cannot even say she is a good religious." Thérèse said: to hear on her deathbed that she was not a good religious — what joy!
- Opposition detail: Two unnamed sisters. Type: disparagement to her face and behind her back. Documented reason (TSA, 1911): the second spoke 'in a fit of bad temper.'

- Confidence: high
- Notes: CONFLICT: in PO Thérèse "heard" the remark herself ("J'entendais aussi"); in PA the words "had been reported" to her. TSA says (PO) the speaker said it "in a fit of bad temper." The first remark ("how little you are loved") is dropped in PA. See F-EW-1719 for the speaker's later fate.

### F-EW-1331 — Last words and last sacraments, as Godefroy heard them
- Claim: Godefroy says that in her agony, eyes on the crucifix, she told her prioress "I look and I love!", and died holding the crucifix saying "My God, my God, yes, I love you." He says the ecclesiastical superior gave her the last sacraments and told him how moved he was by her serenity. She received communion daily as long as she could.
- When / where: 1897, Lisieux Carmel infirmary
- People: Thérèse; her prioress; the superior

- Extraction ID: H-F105
### F-EW-1332 — The grave, the cross, and the 1910 exhumation
- Claim: She was buried in the Lisieux (Saint-Jacques) cemetery. At the time of the Ordinary Process the grave was opened and the remains put in a zinc coffin: only bones remained; the body had undergone "ordinary corruption". The coffin went back to the same place in the Carmel's plot. Before and after, a plain wooden cross like the other nuns' bore her name and "died 17 [sic] September 1897". The Bishop took every care that nothing resembled a public cult at the exhumation.
- When / where: 1897; exhumation 6 September 1910; Lisieux cemetery
- People: Mgr Lemonnier, Bishop of Bayeux
- Witness: Godefroy, "personal knowledge" and public knowledge
- Source: PA-26, f. 35v–37r (54th–55th questions)
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium — the date on the cross is surely wrong (she died 30 September)
- Notes: none
- Extraction ID: H-F108

- Claim: Céline saw her receive more than five hundred cauterisation points (pointes de feu) on the back; Céline counted them. ### F-EW-1370 — The almanac joke: "Che suis riche, drès riche"
- Claim: A novice showed her an almanac caricature of a wealthy Jew boasting he started with nothing; his friend replied that the man he did business with had something. Thérèse repeated it in the accent: she was like this man — she began with nothing, but the One she dealt with had something. She used "we must be rich, very rich both of us" in a note to Céline.
- When / where: 1897
- Witness: Céline — HEARD
- Source: CS §VII "Réminiscences…"

- Claim: In her illness she called Céline "bo-bonne" (little maid), easier to say when exhausted; she asked permission first. She would not use "Céline" — a name so dear to her that a calendar without St Céline (21 Oct) seemed charmless.

- Claim: She compared their course to two children in a Plockhorst picture: a boy in a simple tunic pulls his sister, who resists, loaded with a big bouquet, by a precipice under their guardian angel. ### F-EW-1377 — The chestnut walk: "Even if I die of it, I want to console you"

### F-EW-1401 — 28 May 1897: the painting for Gonzague's feast
- Claim: On 28 May 1897, with Thérèse already very ill, Sr Saint-Jean-Baptiste demanded her immediate help with a painting for Gonzague's feast. Thérèse excused herself, Agnès objected, the sister insisted, and Thérèse grew impatient; the sister left. That evening, instead of passing coldly, she embraced Thérèse: "Poor little sister, you made me feel sorry for you; I was wrong." Thérèse "could not get over it"; that evening God's mercy "overflowed" for her.
- When / where: 28 May 1897

- Witness: Thérèse (her own note, quoted)

- Translation: "Poor little sister, I felt sorry for you, I don't want to tire you, I was wrong"

### F-EW-1405 — Writing Manuscript C in the garden, interrupted at every moment
- Claim: At the end of her life, very ill, she wrote her manuscript in the garden. Pauline saw sisters disturb her every moment. Each time she put down her pen and closed her notebook with a gentle smile. Asked how she could put two ideas together, she said she was writing on fraternal charity, so this was the time to practise it. She said sadly she had not written what she wanted; she needed more solitude, but "the thought is there, you will only have to arrange it".
- When / where: June 1897, garden of the Carmel

- Claim: In June 1897, seeing herself taken by pulmonary tuberculosis, she told Céline that God would take her at an age when she could not have been a priest: had she been one, she would have received Orders at this June ordination; so, that she regret nothing, God let her be ill.

### F-EW-1433 — "She has no merit; she never had struggles"
- Claim: Opposition: TSA heard a sister say that Thérèse had no merit in practising virtue because she had never had struggles. In 1915 she adds that people "did not fear to say it loudly." About two months before Thérèse's death, TSA asked her directly. Thérèse said: "Oh, I have had them! I had a nature that was not easy; it did not show, but I felt it. Not one day without suffering, not one!" In 1915 TSA adds: "Ah, creatures! when they do not see, they do not believe!"
- Opposition detail: Unnamed sister(s) of the community. Type: belittling judgement. Documented reason (TSA): Thérèse's constant peace and ease made people think virtue cost her nothing.
- When / where: c. late July 1897, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; TSA; an unnamed critic
- Witness: TSA, HEARD both the critic and Thérèse
- Source: PO-13 [584r], session 55; PA-09 [835], session 41
- Original words: « J'avais une nature pas commode; cela ne paraissait pas; mais moi, je le sentais bien »
- Translation: I had a difficult nature; it did not show, but I felt it well.
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: A key "hidden temper" statement. Links with Léonie's remark that Thérèse wrote her nature was proud (F-EW-073).
- Extraction ID: F-F24

### F-EW-1436 — "Not only not a saint, not even a good religious"
- Claim: Late July 1897, Thérèse had an overwhelming disgust for food. Sr St Vincent de Paul brought her a cup of meat juice, which Thérèse refused. Back in the kitchen, the lay sister said that Thérèse was not a saint and not even a good religious.

### F-EW-1437 — Mère Agnès tried to catch her in a crisis: "I feel a sharp pain"
- Claim: Two months before her death, Mère Agnès, hearing her patience praised, came to see her hoping to surprise her in a moment of crisis. At that moment Thérèse's face lit with a heavenly smile. Asked why, she said: because I feel a sharp pain; I have always tried to love suffering and welcome it.

### F-EW-1438 — Opposition: "not even a good religious" — the meat-juice cup (July 1897)
- Claim: Opposition: At the end of July 1897 Thérèse had an unconquerable disgust for food. Sr Saint-Vincent de Paul brought her a cup of meat juice; Thérèse refused, for lack of appetite and because the prioress had not ordered it. The sister went back to the kitchen hurt and said Thérèse was not only not a saint but not even a good religious. Told of it, Thérèse rejoiced ("what joy to hear on my deathbed that I am not a good religious"), but was less pleased by the charge that she had known neither suffering nor humiliation: "And I who have suffered so much since my earliest childhood!"
- When / where: late July 1897, infirmary/kitchen
- People: Sr Saint-Vincent de Paul; Thérèse

- Original words: « non seulement n'est pas une sainte mais elle n'est même pas une bonne religieuse »
- Translation: "not only is she no saint, she isn't even a good religious"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: the St-Augustin circular (1929) tells it without naming the critic (K-F183); the St-Augustin biography names Sr Saint-Vincent de Paul. Wording differs slightly.

- Claim: On 6 July 1897 new haemorrhages began. The doctor found a very serious lung congestion, forbade all movement, and prescribed ice, mustard poultices, cupping glasses and more. ### F-EW-1489 — The child's toy of 29 July 1897
- Claim: On 29 July 1897, very ill in the infirmary, a sister brought her a small child's toy to amuse her. Surprised, Thérèse accepted it without enthusiasm, saying "What do you want me to do with that?" The sister, a little hurt, told her she was not being gracious. Thérèse answered: "You are right, oh how imperfect I am!... but I am happy to feel so wretched." Despite exhaustion she then welcomed all visitors with a smile.

### F-EW-1510 — Liturgy: Offices of the Dead
- Claim: The Carmelite Constitutions required each nun to say Offices of the Dead for every deceased member of the Order. In May 1897 Thérèse begged not to be dispensed; later she was dispensed. On 6 Aug she asked God to pay her debt to the souls in purgatory "as God", recalling St John of the Cross's line "Acquittez toutes dettes".
- When / where: May–Aug 1897, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès
- Witness: Mère Agnès; HEARD
- Source: PO 1, NV f. 248v; f. 273v
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: A-F180
### F-EW-1511 — The Office: "my happiness and my martyrdom" because of mistakes
- Claim: She was happy and proud to be hebdomadary, saying the prayers aloud in the choir like the priest, and to read the Gospel as first chantress. She said the Divine Office was both her happiness and her martyrdom, because she wanted to say it without error but sometimes made mistakes despite her care.
- When / where: Carmel; said August 1897
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: Mère Agnès, HEARD
- Source: PA 6, [381], S12 6 Jul 1915
- New to English readers: partial (Last Conversations)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: B-F57
### F-EW-1512 — She rejected a priest's claim that the Virgin did not suffer physically
- Claim: A priest wrote to the Carmel that the Virgin did not know bodily suffering. Thérèse said that looking at Mary's statue that evening she understood this was false: Mary suffered from travel, cold, heat, tiredness and fasting. She liked to imagine the Holy Family's life as very ordinary, not full of the marvels people tell.
- When / where: August 1897
- People: Thérèse; an unnamed priest
- Witness: Mère Agnès, HEARD
- Source: PA 6, [387], S13 7 Jul 1915
- New to English readers: partial (Last Conversations)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: B-F61

- Claim: In the infirmary they guessed at last that she suffered greatly in one shoulder and wanted to ease it. She said to leave her "little shoulder pain", because it made her think of Jesus carrying the cross.

- Claim: During Dr de Cornière's five weeks of holiday, the mother prioress let Dr La Néele in only three times, although he declared she needed a doctor every day.

- Claim: She suffered much in the side, the shoulder, and from a burning thirst nothing could quench. She said that when she drank, it was like pouring fire on fire.

### F-EW-1516 — "Don't leave poison near patients in such pain"
- Claim: She said that if she had no faith she would despair; she understood well why people without faith kill themselves when they suffer so much. She told Pauline: when you have patients in such violent pain, be careful not to leave near them remedies that are poison; it takes only a moment, when suffering like this, to lose one's reason.
- When / where: late August 1897
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès
- Witness: Mère Agnès, HEARD
- Source: PA 6, [505], S19 15 Jul 1915

- Claim: A sister in the infirmary tried everyone's patience with her many odd habits. When the others showed how tiresome it was to keep her company, Thérèse said she would have been happy to be asked to do it.

### F-EW-1518 — "Too little to be damned"; "not a saint"; the superior and her crown
- Claim: When Céline quoted a saint who feared damnation to his last breath, Thérèse said she was too small to be damned; little children are never damned. When Céline called her a saint she said no, she had never done saints' deeds; she was a very little soul God had filled with graces. When "our father superior" told her she was not going to heaven soon, her crown was only begun, she answered that God had made it, not she.
- When / where: July–Aug 1897, infirmary
- People: Céline; Thérèse; the ecclesiastical superior (unnamed; Canon Maupas in 1897, not named here)
- Witness: Céline — HEARD
- Source: PO 4, f. 397v, S33

- Translation: "I'm too little to be damned; little children are never damned"

- Confidence: high

### F-EW-1519 — The box of lilies; the candle and holy water; laughing at her own funeral
- Claim: She asked to see a box of lilies that had arrived to decorate her bier and looked at them with pleasure: "It's for me!" To please her they discussed the new cemetery in front of her. One evening, when she might not survive the night, a blessed candle and holy water with sprinkler were put in the next room; she guessed, asked that they be placed where she could see them, then described with joy every detail of her burial, in terms that made the sisters laugh when they wanted to cry. She said: "When I think I am dying in a bed! I'd have liked to die in an arena!"
- When / where: July–Aug 1897, infirmary
- People: Thérèse; her sisters
- Witness: Céline — SAW and HEARD
- Source: PO 4, f. 398v, S33
- Original words: « qui nous faisaient rire quand nous aurions voulu pleurer »
- Translation: "which made us laugh when we would have wanted to cry"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Céline: "it was not we who encouraged her, but she who cheered us".
- Extraction ID: C-F210
### F-EW-1520 — "You know you are nursing a little saint"
- Claim: About six weeks before her death, with Marie, Mother Agnès and Sr Geneviève at her bed, Thérèse, without any lead-in, looked at them with a heavenly air and said very clearly that they knew they were nursing a little saint. Marie was so moved she moved away in the infirmary and did not hear anything else.
- When / where: infirmary, about mid-August 1897
- People: Thérèse; Marie; Mother Agnès; Sr Geneviève
- Witness: Marie, heard it
- Source: PA 7, [605]–[606], Session 25
- Original words: « Vous savez bien que vous soignez une petite sainte »
- Translation: "You know very well you are nursing a little saint."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for the words; the tribunal asked if she explained or corrected them; Marie did not hear
- Notes: The tribunal's question shows it worried about pride. Other sources give a follow-up explanation; Marie did not hear it.
- Extraction ID: D-F78

- Claim: About "You know you are nursing a little saint", Céline thinks she heard it directly, since she seldom left her, but she did not note it in her notebook and admits she may have known it only from her sisters.

### F-EW-1522 — Late August 1897: could not bear the rustle of paper
- Claim: By 27 Aug 1897 she was weaker and could not stand the least noise near her, even paper rustling or whispered words. Her state had changed much since the Assumption (15 Aug). The sisters came to wish for her release, as she suffered "martyrdom". The oppression (breathlessness) made her suffer greatly.
- When / where: late Aug 1897, infirmary
- Witness: Marie de l'Eucharistie, letter of 27 Aug 1897
- Source: PO-20 [1152r]
- Original words: « même le froissement du papier, ou quelques paroles dites à voix basse »
- Translation: "even the rustling of paper, or a few words spoken softly"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high — contemporary letter
- Notes: none
- Extraction ID: G-F28
### F-EW-1523 — Gonzague refused Dr La Néele in the crisis a month before death
- Claim: About a month before her death Thérèse went through a very painful crisis with no relief. The community doctor was on holiday. The sisters asked the prioress to let Dr La Néele in; she refused flatly. Thérèse stayed in cruel torment without relief. When her sisters complained, she told them not to murmur: it was God who allowed their Mother to give her no relief.
- When / where: about late Aug 1897; infirmary
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Dr Francis La Néele (cousin by marriage); community doctor (Dr de Cornière, not named here); Thérèse; her sisters
- Witness: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, participant in the request
- Source: MSC notes (URL at F-EW-567), FOI
- Original words: « Mes petites sœurs il ne faut pas murmurer contre la volonté du Bon Dieu »
- Translation: "My little sisters, you must not grumble against God's will"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high for the refusal as the sisters experienced it
- Notes: Thérèse de St Augustin also says that "in the last months" the prioress thought she should refuse the means to relieve her (F-EW-1612). Marie des Anges instead blames the doctor for not thinking of palliatives (F-EW-1338). Conflict on who was responsible.
- Extraction ID: I-F46

- Claim: She dozed off for a second during prayer and dreamed that soldiers were lacking for a war against the Prussians; Pauline said "send Sr Thérèse". She agreed, though she preferred a holy war. - Claim: Differences between Bible translations saddened her; she said that if she had been a priest she would have studied Hebrew and Greek thoroughly to know God's thought. - Claim: When she said she had refused God nothing since age three, Céline cried: you see, you are a saint! She answered quickly: no, I am not a saint; I have never done the actions of the saints; I am a very little soul God has filled with graces; you will see in heaven I speak the truth.

- Claim: Replying to a confidence from Pauline, she said a prioress should always let others think she has no troubles, and should avoid saying things like "you have problems, I have them too with such-and-such a sister".

- Claim: Distinctive: She told her sisters not to be surprised if she did not appear to them after death or if they saw nothing extraordinary, because her "little way" was to desire to see nothing. She said images of angels did her no good; she could feed only on truth, so she never desired visions.

### F-EW-1536 — Pride as hebdomadary: saying prayers aloud like a priest
- Claim: She said she was proud when she was hebdomadary (semainière) at the Office, saying the prayers aloud in the middle of choir, because the priest said the same prayers at Mass and she had, like him, the right to speak aloud before the Blessed Sacrament, give blessings and absolutions, and read the Gospel as first chantress. - Claim: About things confided to her, she said few religious are perfect; many act carelessly, telling themselves they are not bound; those who do everything as well as possible are rare. She stressed silence above all, since it is the point most failed.

- Claim: She had no outward attacks of the devil, except once, a whole night, which she revealed to Céline. In the morning Céline found her pale and disfigured by suffering and anguish. ### F-EW-1621 — Mère Hermance, "old Father Corbinelli", and the two graves
- Claim: During Thérèse's last illness, the life of St Aloysius Gonzaga was read in the refectory. Mère Hermance, struck by his friendship with the old Fr Corbinelli, told Thérèse: you are little Louis, I am old Corbinelli; remember me in heaven. Thérèse offered to come for her soon; Hermance said not yet, she had not suffered enough; she needed authority's sanction for so grave a matter. In the last days, thanking her for flowers, Thérèse sent word that at Mass that morning she had seen Corbinelli's grave very close to little Louis's. Hermance said she had understood. Their graves are indeed side by side in Lisieux cemetery.

### F-EW-1644 — Last words and acts, 29 Sept 1897
- Claim: On 29 Sept from morning she seemed in agony, with a painful rattle. At noon she asked the prioress: "Mother, is this the agony? How shall I die? I'll never know how to die!" Pauline read her, in French, the office of St Michael and the prayers for the dying; at the mention of demons she made a childlike threatening gesture and smiled. After the doctor's visit she asked "Is it today, Mother?"; told yes, and that God was joyful, she said "Me too!" In the afternoon: "I can't go on! Let people pray for me!" After Matins she joined her hands: "Yes, my God, I accept everything!"

- People: Thérèse; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mère Agnès; Dr de Cornière (visit)

### F-EW-1651 — The agony began at 4.30 p.m.; death a little after 7
- Claim: Her agony began about 4.30 p.m.; she thanked the community with a smile; cold sweat; trembling; a few minutes after seven she asked the prioress, "Is not this the agony? Am I not going to die?"; "Yes … but perhaps Jesus wishes to prolong it for several hours." "Well, very well … I do not wish to suffer less." ### F-EW-1652 — The death day: timeline and symptoms
- Claim: On 30 Sept 1897 Pauline watched her during morning Mass; she was exhausted and panting. All day she could still move and sit up. About 3 pm she stretched her arms in a cross; the prioress laid a picture of Our Lady of Mount Carmel on her knees. About 5 pm Pauline was alone with her when her face changed and the agony began. For over two hours a terrible rattle tore her chest; her face was congested, her hands purple, her feet icy; she shook in all her limbs; heavy sweat ran down her face; she gave small involuntary cries to breathe.
- When / where: 30 Sept 1897, infirmary
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès; Mère Marie de Gonzague; the community
- Witness: Mère Agnès; SAW
- Source: PO 1, NV f. 290r–292r, Session 19, 2 Sept 1910; summary f. 246r–v

- Confidence: high — eyewitness notes
- Notes: —

- Claim: Just after 7 pm the prioress, thinking her state was stable, dismissed the community. Thérèse asked if this was not the agony and if she was not going to die. Told it might last some hours, she said "Well… come on… come on…", looked at her crucifix and said "Oh, I love Him… My God, I love you!" She fell gently back, head leaning right. The prioress hurriedly called the community back.

- Claim: After falling back, her face, purple and distorted during the agony, recovered its lily colour; her eyes were fixed upward, shining. A sister brought a candle close to see her gaze; her eyelids did not move. This lasted about the length of a Creed. In the oral account Pauline saw her close her eyes, sigh several times, and die. In the NV notebook the candle-bearer is named: Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie.

### F-EW-1656 — Last words, 30 Sept 1897: morning to 3 pm
- Claim: In the morning, while Pauline watched her during Mass, she said nothing, panting; then, joining her hands and looking at the Virgin's statue facing her bed: "Oh, I prayed to her so fervently! But it's pure agony, with no consolation." During the day, able to sit up, she said she felt strong, did not think she would die, and cried: "If this is the agony, what is death?", "the cup is full to the brim", "My little sisters, pray for me!" About 3 pm she put her arms in a cross; the prioress placed an image of Our Lady of Mount Carmel on her knees. She said: "Mother, present me quickly to the Blessed Virgin; prepare me to die well."

### F-EW-1657 — Last words, 30 Sept 1897: 3 pm to death
- Claim: When the prioress said her humility was her preparation, Thérèse said: "Yes, it seems to me I never sought anything but the truth. Yes, I understood humility of heart." Then: all she wrote about desiring suffering "is really true"; proudly, "I do not regret having given myself up to Love"; "I would never have believed it possible to suffer so much… I can only explain it by my burning desire to save souls." About 5 pm, Pauline alone with her, the agony began; she greeted the community with a smile, crucifix in hand. At 6 pm Angelus she looked up at the Virgin's statue. After 7 pm: "Mother, isn't this the agony yet? Am I not going to die?"; then "Well… come on… come on… I wouldn't want to suffer less long"; eyes on the crucifix: "Oh… I love Him… My… God… I… love… you!!"
- When / where: 30 Sept 1897, 3 pm to c. 7:20 pm, infirmary
- People: Thérèse; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mère Agnès; Sr Geneviève; Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie; the community
- Witness: Mère Agnès; SAW and HEARD
- Source: PO 1, NV f. 291r–292v; f. 246r–v, Session 19, 2 Sept 1910
- Original words: « Oh!... je l'aime!... Mon... Dieu! je... vous... aime!! »

- Notes: Pauline says that from 3 pm "it seemed it was no longer she who suffered"; she thought of martyrs given to executioners.

- Claim: On the morning of 30 September Pauline stayed with her during Mass. - Claim: In the afternoon she revived and kept begging prayers: "My God, have pity on me", "I will all of it", even months or years of this. - Claim: About 4:30, Pauline guessed from a sudden pallor that the end was near, and the community gathered. She held her crucifix so tightly that it was hard to take it from her after death. Her face and hands, first deathly pale, turned red-violet. Sr Geneviève came to wipe the sweat running from her forehead; Thérèse thanked her with a smile and a look.

- Claim: As the agony went on, the prioress sent the community away about 7 p.m. Thérèse sighed: "Am I not going to die then? Is it not the agony yet?" Told she might have a few hours more, she moaned gently: "Well… come… come… Oh, I would not want to suffer less!"

- Claim: Her breathing became weaker and faster; she fell back on the pillow, head leaning to the right. The infirmary bell rang. Hardly had the sisters knelt round the bed when she said clearly, looking at her crucifix, "Oh! I love him!" and a moment later "My God… I… love you!"

### F-EW-1663 — The last gaze; candle before her eyes; death at 7:20
- Claim: They thought all was over when she suddenly raised her eyes, full of life and fire, showing happiness. Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie, wanting to see this look closer, passed a candle back and forth before her eyes; the lids did not flicker. It lasted about the length of a Creed. Pauline calls it an ecstasy that broke her chain. It was 7:20 p.m. Then she closed her eyes, and her face, white during the ecstasy, became whiter and more matte, very beautiful, with a smile.

### F-EW-1664 — She closed her own eyes; laid out by Aimée de Jésus, Marie and Pauline; looked 12
- Claim: No one had to close her eyes; she closed them herself after the ecstasy. The prioress sent the community out. Sr Aimée de Jésus, Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur and Pauline laid out the body. Her face looked like a child's; she seemed about 12. Dressed and laid on her straw mattress (Carmel custom), she had her crucifix, rosary and a palm in her hand, and nearby on a small table the statue of the miraculous Virgin. Her head still leaned to the right, and her smile was so marked she seemed only asleep.

### F-EW-1692 — The death: MdA's two accounts
- Claim: (1911) In the afternoon of 30 Sept the community gathered; agony began at 4:30. Thérèse thanked the community with a smile, held the crucifix, cold sweat on her face, trembling in all limbs. My God, I love you." Suddenly she raised her head "with a quite strange force," opened her eyes wide and fixed a magnificent look above the statue of the Virgin; then her head fell back. MdA thought she saw Our Lord. ### F-EW-2551 — TSA's dream of 8 Jan 1897: 'Sister Thérèse is called — she must be very beautiful'
- Claim: TSA dreamed on 8 Jan 1897 that she was in a very dark room closed by a heavy black door with a line of dazzling light under it. From the next room a voice said: 'Sister Thérèse of the Child Jesus is called!' She felt that beside her, in an even darker part, her little sister was being prepared. The voice insisted: 'She must be very beautiful!' She woke sure that Thérèse would soon leave for heaven. Some weeks later in recreation she told Thérèse. Thérèse was deeply moved and said God had sent the dream to encourage her through TSA: the dark room and black door were the image of her darkness; the line of light and the call were a consoling sign. Thérèse insisted on the words 'through you', with tears in her eyes, and told her of her trial of faith, which TSA had not known.
- When / where: 8 Jan 1897; Feb–Mar 1897, Lisieux Carmel
- People: TSA; Thérèse
- Witness: Mère Agnès de Jésus (circular writer), drawing on TSA's own notes and stories
- Source: Circular (obituary notice) of Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin (Julia Leroyer, 1856–1929), written by Mère Agnès de Jésus, dated 8 Sep 1929, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/au-carmel-du-temps-de-therese/la-communaute/soeur-therese-de-st-augustin/circulaire-de-soeur-therese-de-st-augustin/ — circular, paragraphs 'Le Seigneur, qui devait se complaire…' and 'Le 8 janvier 1897…'
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — TSA's own account, as retold by Agnès
- Notes: A surprise: TSA says she knew nothing of Thérèse's trial of faith until this talk. Thérèse's trial began at Easter 1896 (Ms C), so it had lasted about ten months. Compare F-EW-1131 (TSA's testimony on the trial of faith).
- Extraction ID: N-F104
### F-EW-2560 — The little niece 'Madeleine' and Thérèse's picture of meeting God (6 July 1897)
- Claim: The biography says that Sr Marie-Élisabeth's niece, a little girl called 'Madeleine', once recited a small compliment to the nuns at the parlour grille, ending with a curtsy and 'Happiness for all those I love'. Thérèse told this to her sisters on 6 July 1897: when she is in heaven she will go up to God like the little niece: he will say 'What do you want, little girl?' and she will answer 'Happiness for all those I love'; she will do the same before all the saints. The page cites Derniers Entretiens 6/7/1897. The circular calls the niece 'petite nièce' and Marie-Élisabeth 'Tante Babeth'.
- When / where: 6 Jul 1897 (words); Lisieux Carmel

### F-EW-2561 — 26 Sept 1897: Sr Marie-Élisabeth sits with the dying Thérèse — and exhausts her
- Claim: On Sunday 26 Sep 1897, while the community was at Mass, Sr Marie-Élisabeth was told to watch Thérèse. Sure she was with a saint, she stayed the whole hour asking questions and admiring the answers. A painful suffocation crisis followed after she left. She never knew this and kept only a heavenly memory. She was struck that Thérèse would not let her try to kill the flies that troubled her in the heat: she begged her not to harm them because they were her only enemies and she was glad to have a chance to forgive.

### F-EW-2562 — Burial on 4 Oct 1897, the exhumations, and the 'Non-Cult' process: what the extern sisters saw
- Claim: Sr Marie-Élisabeth followed Thérèse's small cortege to the cemetery on 4 Oct 1897, which the biography calls very modest for a young nun who had 'lived little'. She was present at the exhumation of 6 Sep 1910 (end of the diocesan process) and again on 10 Aug 1917 (end of the Apostolic Process), and testified about them. Questioned in the 'Non-Cult' process, she spoke of the growing devotion of the faithful and showed that no public or official cult was given: she and her companions often climbed to the cemetery to remove the ex-votos, petitions, candles and flowers that pilgrims left, and brought everything back to the Carmel, to avoid ostentation until the Church judged.

### F-EW-2616 — 2 June 1897: Marie's veiling, Thérèse's note, and the order to keep writing
- Claim: Marie took the veil on 2 Jun 1897, sixteen years to the day after her First Communion, after a sermon by Abbé Levasseur. Piat calls it 'almost a testament'. ### F-EW-2618 — Bulletin of 7–8 July 1897: 'Dying will be my happiness; living too'; the doctor's verdict; the move to the infirmary
- Claim: Piat quotes what Thérèse said (as Marie reported) three or four days after Marie's veiling: 'Dying will be my happiness; living will be so too, because I want only what God wants'; 'Either the Blessed Virgin will heal me or take me. It cannot last long.' On 7 Jul Dr de Cornière diagnosed a right-lung congestion with tubercular flare-ups; haemorrhages and fever rose, which made Thérèse say 'in Purgatory they cannot burn more'. Marie wrote: 'When one goes to see her she is much changed, very thin, but always the same calm and the joke. She sees death coming with happiness and has not the least fear.' Thérèse told them, on Purgatory: 'You do God a great injury believing you will go to Purgatory. When one loves there can be no Purgatory.' On the evening of 8 Jul, during a lull, she was carried to the infirmary facing the statue of the Virgin of the Smile.

### F-EW-2621 — Bulletin of 9 July 1897: 'gay as a chaffinch'; 'one of the two in a hundred'; 'a little girl of contradiction'
- Claim: Marie wrote to her parents: 'If you saw our dear little patient you could not help laughing; she always has something amusing to say. Since she believes she is dying she is gay as a chaffinch. There are moments when one would pay for a seat beside her.' That morning Thérèse said 'If I were to be one of the two!... one of the two in a hundred! Would that not be unfortunate!' because Mother Agnès had told her that Dr de Cornière said only two of a hundred in her state would survive; she feared to be one of those who can be saved. To Marie's promise to write to reassure them: 'Tell them I love them and that I am a little girl of contradiction: they think me dying and I do not yet turn my eyes; they think me alive and I am nearly dead; I am pure contradiction; but above all tell them I love them very very much.'

### F-EW-2622 — Canon Maupas: 'You are not dying; the sacrament would not be valid' — and Thérèse's comic reply
- Claim: In the 9 Jul bulletin, Marie reported that the Carmel's ecclesiastical superior, Canon Maupas (curé of Saint-Jacques), visited that morning and said: 'You want to make us believe... you are not dying, and soon you will run in the garden; you do not have the face of a dying woman. Give you Extreme Unction? The sacrament would not be valid; you are not ill enough.' After he left Thérèse said: 'Another time I will not go to so much trouble to be polite: I sat on our bed, I was pleasant, and he refuses what I ask! Another time I will use 'pretence': I will drink a cup of milk before his arrival, because I always look worse after; then I will scarcely answer him, saying that I am in agony.' Marie says 'she was literally playing the comedy for us... Yes, I see that I do not know my trade; I do not know how to go about it.'

### F-EW-2624 — 12 July 1897: 'a bed of misfortune — one always misses the train'; 'the thief has gone far away'
- Claim: Marie wrote that on 12 Jul Thérèse had been lowered into the infirmary bed of Mother Geneviève, who had also several times wanted and been disappointed of death. Thérèse said: 'What a bed of misfortune! When you are in it you always miss the train.' Then: 'The thief has gone far away, he left me to steal other children. When will it be my turn? I do not know now.' And: 'Tell my dear little Uncle, my Aunt, Léonie, all, that when I am in Heaven what will give me the most happiness will be to be able then to express all my love to them. I cannot on earth; my love is too strong; in Heaven I will make them understand. That is my joy.' Asked what she would say on seeing God for the first time: 'Do not speak of it, I cannot think of it, it makes me so happy. What I will do... I will weep for joy.'

### F-EW-2625 — The Guérins on Thérèse's dying: 'I will engrave all she says and does'
- Claim: On 12 Jul 1897 Mme Guérin wrote to Marie: 'We are in admiration of all that is told us of her. But that is not enough: we must imitate her. And how to reach such detachment, such happiness in dying? Until now I had only read that in the lives of the saints. Today we have the example before us.' Later she wrote that Thérèse entered Carmel 'with her baptismal innocence'. Isidore wrote: 'I see her with her little angelic face waiting for death with joy... I am in deepest admiration before her supernatural wisdom... ### F-EW-2626 — Thérèse's farewell letter to the Guérins, 16 July 1897, and the verse Marie sang before her Communion
- Claim: On the feast of Our Lady of Mount Carmel, 16 Jul 1897, Thérèse wrote her farewell letter to her adoptive parents: she wanted to tell them about her Communion that morning, made 'so touching, or rather so triumphant, by your flowered sheaves'. She left Marie to give the details and said only that Marie sang, before Communion, a small verse Thérèse had composed for that morning ('You who know my extreme littleness, you do not fear to lower yourself to me! Jesus, hear the cry of my tenderness'), and, after Communion, a verse of 'Vivre d'amour': 'To die of love is a sweet martyrdom.' ### F-EW-2628 — Marie's letter of 20 July 1897 on Thérèse's holiness: 'It is not extraordinary sanctity; it is love'
- Claim: On 20 Jul 1897 Marie wrote to her cousin Céline Maudelonde: 'It is not an extraordinary sanctity, not a love of extraordinary penances, no, it is love of God. People of the world can imitate her holiness, for she strove only to do everything out of love and to accept all the small contrarieties, all the small sacrifices that come at every moment, as from God's hand. She sees God in everything and does all her actions as perfectly as possible. Always duty before all! And pleasure she knew how to sanctify, while tasting it, by offering it to God.' She added: 'I asked her the other day: Have you ever refused God anything? She answered: I do not remember. Even when I was very small, from the age of three, I began to refuse God nothing of what he asked. That says everything, does it not?'

### F-EW-2631 — 30 July 1897: Extreme Unction and Viaticum, and 'the day of my death will be the greatest feast'
- Claim: Marie's letter of 30 Jul 1897 describes an acute crisis with frequent haemorrhages, the back 'burning like fire', and oppression so strong that even ether inhalations did not stop the feeling of suffocation. The day before, the sisters had thought that Louis Martin would come for his 'Little Queen' on the third anniversary of his death (29 Jul). On the doctor's advice Thérèse received Extreme Unction and Viaticum on the 30th at 6 p.m. Marie wrote: 'It was touching to see our little patient always with her calm and pure look. When she asked pardon of the whole community, more than one melted into tears... It is impossible to imagine her happiness in dying; it is like a little child who desires with all her heart to go and find her Father... Why would death frighten me? I have never acted except for God.' When told she might die on a feast day: 'I have no need to choose a feast day to die; the day of my death will be the greatest of all feast days for me.'
- When / where: 29–30 Jul 1897, Lisieux Carmel infirmary
- People: Thérèse; Marie Guérin; Louis Martin (memory)
- Witness: Fr Piat (1967), quoting Marie's letters and Thérèse's sayings as Marie recorded them; modern author
- Source: Extract from Fr Stéphane-Joseph Piat OFM, biography of Marie Guérin (Office Central de Lisieux, 1967), on the Carmel site, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/au-carmel-du-temps-de-therese/la-communaute/soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/biographie-de-soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/ — section 'Au lit d'agonie d'une sainte'; rights: facts-only
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high
- Notes: The date of the sacrament, 30 July 1897 at 6 p.m., is consistent with other cards. The 'greatest of all feast days' remark is not in the main collection of last words here.
- Extraction ID: N-F182
### F-EW-2632 — 'I believe she will die laughing': Thérèse tells the sisters what will happen after her death
- Claim: Marie wrote that Thérèse 'amuses herself telling us all that will happen after her death. From the way she tells it, where one should weep, one laughs aloud, so amusing is she... She reviews everything, it is her happiness, and shares it in forms that make us laugh a lot. I think she will die laughing, she is so gay.' The threat then seeming to pass, M. and Mme Guérin took their annual cure at Vichy. At the Carmel Gonzague's name day was celebrated four days early so Thérèse could take part, and it was Thérèse who handed the prioress, with a charming mime, the gift her aunt brought from Évreux.
- When / where: 30 Jul – Aug 1897, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Marie Guérin; Gonzague; M. and Mme Guérin
- Witness: Fr Piat (1967), quoting Marie's letters and Thérèse's sayings as Marie recorded them; modern author

### F-EW-2633 — 16 Aug 1897: Dr La Néele examines Thérèse; 'if they told me I would recover I would be as content'
- Claim: On 15 Aug 1897 a violent left-side pain and swollen legs (heart failure signs) caused new fear. On 16 Aug Dr Francis La Néele, passing through Lisieux, was allowed to examine his young cousin. Marie wrote: 'This visit moved him. He found Thérèse very ill and gives her hardly more than fifteen days... he said tuberculosis had reached its last degree... he found our little patient wonderfully cared for, and said that with all the care Dr de Cornière had given, if she had not recovered it was because God wanted to take her.' She added that Thérèse said that morning: 'If they told me I would get well, do not think I would be caught out. I would be as content as to die. I have a great desire for Heaven but above all I am happy because I am in great peace; to feel an immense joy as sometimes when the heart beats with happiness, oh no!... I am in peace; that is why I am happy.'
- When / where: 15–16 Aug 1897, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Dr Francis La Néele; Marie Guérin; Thérèse; Dr de Cornière
- Witness: Fr Piat (1967), quoting Marie's letters and Thérèse's sayings as Marie recorded them; modern author

### F-EW-2634 — Isidore's letter from Vichy, 18 Aug 1897: the Casino against the Carmel
- Claim: From Vichy on 18 Aug 1897, Isidore Guérin wrote to his niece, contrasting the spa town, with its Casino, Eden theatre and ball, with the modest Carmel 'without gilding or sculpture, from which rises the murmur of prayer... In front of the bare cross'. 'On a bed of pain lies a pure young girl, already consumed by the fires of that divine love to which she aspires to be entirely and forever joined. She does not desire death but loves it as a liberator. She asks for suffering to be more like her Master, and offers all... not in expiation of the sins she has committed but for those of that crowd that runs dancing, to the sound of infernal violins, to throw itself gaily into hell. And I heard the very sweet voices of my five little girls crying to heaven: Mercy, Lord! Pardon!' He thanked God for 'such angels'.

### F-EW-2635 — 22 Aug 1897: Thérèse cannot answer her uncle; 'What a good man my Uncle is'
- Claim: On 22 Aug 1897 (Marie's birthday) Thérèse gave Marie a picture she wished to sign, which took all her strength; she thought she could not finish. She could not answer her uncle's letter, which grieved her: 'You will not be able to express all I feel; you will not tell them enough how I love them and how touched I am by their affection.' She never tired of hearing the letter; Marie had to read it several times; 'Oh, how good my Uncle is! What a great soul he has!' To say those few words she had to stop a minute between each word because of oppression. When the Eucharist was brought, the sisters entered chanting the Miserere; the last time she was so weak that hearing it made her suffer 'martyrdom'.

### F-EW-2637 — 27 Aug 1897: 'Happily I did not ask for suffering'; 'let all the prayers be for sinners'
- Claim: Marie wrote on 27 Aug 1897 that Thérèse was weaker, could not bear any noise, even the rustling of paper or whispers; the state had changed much since the Assumption and the sisters had come to desire her release. She told them the previous day: 'Fortunately I did not ask for suffering, for if I had asked it, I would fear not to have patience to bear it; whereas, coming from the pure will of God, he cannot refuse me patience and grace.' Oppression made her suffer, but the worst torture was bowel pains. Yet she said: 'I ask that all the prayers made for me should not serve to lighten my sufferings but be all for sinners.' From this time the letters became rare and news was given orally at the turn or parlour.

### F-EW-2639 — 11 Sept 1897: 'Be very gentle — never a hard word' — the case of Sister X
- Claim: On 11 Sep 1897 Thérèse warned Marie: 'You must become very gentle. Never hard words, never a hard tone; never take a hard air, be always gentle. Yesterday you hurt Sister X... A few moments later another sister hurt her too. What happened? She wept. If you had not treated her harshly she would have accepted the second pain better, and it would have passed unnoticed. But two pains so close together put her in great sadness, whereas if you had been gentle nothing would have happened.' Piat says she stayed 'the watcher' looking for her novices' failings and 'the little brush' painting the image of the perfect religious on their souls until the end. Her last message to her father, relayed on 17 Sep: 'If I thought I was well loved, I would never have believed they loved me so much!'
- When / where: 11 and 17 Sep 1897, Lisieux Carmel infirmary
- People: Thérèse; Marie Guérin; 'Sister X' (unnamed)

### F-EW-2641 — 4 Oct 1897 burial: Isidore Guérin had bought the cemetery plot; depth 3.20 m
- Claim: Piat says the burial took place on Monday 4 Oct 1897, the feast of St Francis, with a small cortege including several priests. The 'Little Queen' was buried in that enclosure at a depth of 3.20 metres. Crowds later came to pray there, until the triumphal translation to the Carmel on 26 Mar 1923 for the Beatification.

### F-LF-230 — The infirmary: nothing "worth recounting"
- Claim: Laveille says that while she lay in her cell after cautery during recreation, she heard a sister in the kitchen say, "Sœur Thérèse… will soon die, and in truth, I ask myself what can our Mother say of her after her death? She will be embarrassed, for this little Sister, amiable though she is, has surely done nothing worth recounting." (The prioress writes a circular for each dead nun.) Thérèse said to the infirmarian, "Ah, happily the good God has always given me the grace of being absolutely indifferent to [the opinion of creatures]." Another lay sister, temporary infirmarian, offered her food that would have made her sick; Thérèse refused gently. The sister said afterwards, "I do not know why they talk so much of Sœur Thérèse; she does nothing remarkable; one cannot even say that she is exactly a good religious." Thérèse smiled: "To hear on my bed of death that I am not a good religious, what joy!"
- When / where: 1897; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; unnamed nuns
- Source: LAV p.344, p.372–373
- Source type: eyewitness
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — nuns unnamed; the "circular" note is Laveille's.
- Notes: Surprise: the community did not expect a story to tell.
### F-LF-231 — Novissima Verba: Mère Agnès takes notes
- Claim: From 6 Apr 1897 Mère Agnès wrote in a notebook what struck her in their talks. Thérèse at first felt uneasy and then agreed. Laveille lists the themes (the little way, conformity to God's will, the privileges of Mary, the Holy Family's simplicity, communion of saints). On 1 Aug 1897 Mère Agnès said she meant to have the manuscript read in community and printed. Thérèse said: "My Mother, after my death, my manuscript should not be spoken of to anyone until it is published… the demon will set many snares." She also asked Mère Agnès to revise an incomplete passage, and was found in tears: "these pages will do a great deal of good… Ah, I know it, everyone will love me." The Carmel adds that in the night of 2 Jun 1897 Mère Agnès asked Marie de Gonzague to have Thérèse write more, and from 3–4 Jun Thérèse wrote Ms C in a black notebook.
- When / where: Apr–Aug 1897; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Mère Agnès; Thérèse; Marie de Gonzague
- Source: LAV p.368–369, p.374; Carmel "la fabrication" page (June 1897)

- Rights: public-domain / unknown

### F-LF-232 — Farewell to her sisters, and the hen with her chicks
- Claim: On 4 Jun 1897, with her three sisters in her cell, she bade farewell, "radiant… as if transfigured": "how happy I am! I see that I am soon to die… I would wish, however, to have an easy death in order to console you. But do not feel disturbed if I suffer greatly." On 7 Jun, returning from the garden with Mère Agnès, she saw a white hen with chicks under her wings, and wept. "Our Saviour… has entirely hidden me under His wings." Marie asked, "What a sorrow it will be for us when you die!" and she said, "Oh no… there will be as it were a shower of roses."
- When / where: 4–7 Jun 1897; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Marie; Pauline; Céline

### F-LF-233 — Moved to the infirmary, 8 July 1897
- Claim: Because fresh haemorrhages might kill her suddenly, she was moved on 8 Jul 1897 to the infirmary. Leaving her cell she told Mère Agnès: "I have suffered greatly here; I would have liked to die here." On a table by the bed stood the statue of the Virgin of the Smile. Marie asked, "What do you see?" She answered: "Never has it appeared so beautiful to me; but today it is the statue; before, as you well know, it was not the statue." A haemorrhage came on 4 Jul. Marie de Gonzague had let her sleep in the cell rather than the infirmary because her cough would disturb no one. Relatives could not visit in the enclosure, so she wrote farewells. On 16 Jul, feast of Our Lady of Mount Carmel, a young priest said his first Mass in the chapel and brought the Host to the infirmary along cloisters strewn with wild flowers and rose petals; Marie Guérin sang her verse "To die of love". On 17 Jul she told Mère Agnès: "I WILL SPEND MY HEAVEN IN DOING GOOD UPON EARTH."
- When / where: 4–17 Jul 1897; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès; Marie; Marie Guérin; an unnamed young priest
- Source: LAV p.375, p.378–383 (Summarium 1919 p.893 §2585)
- Source type: eyewitness
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial (baseline quotes the "heaven" line)
- Confidence: high
### F-LF-234 — Letters to the family in July 1897: Léonie, and Isidore's reply
- Claim: On 17 Jul 1897 she wrote to Léonie: "I do not wish to enter heaven a minute sooner by my own will… If you wish to be a saint, that will be easy for you, because in your heart the world is nothing to you." On 16 Jul 1897 she wrote in pencil to the Guérins, promising cousin Jeanne (childless) a "cherub" or else "mother of many children" (souls); "True I am like a lark except when the fever attacks me; happily, it only comes at night, when larks sleep." Isidore replied on 25 Jul 1897: "You were your good mother's precious pearl, last in coming, your venerated father's 'little queen'… It is said that the swan, though silent during its whole life, breaks into a sublime song as death approaches. Your letter… is, without doubt, your last song for us… He who has perhaps some small claim to be called your second father embraces you with deep affection. I. Guérin."
- When / where: 16–25 Jul 1897; Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; Léonie; Isidore and Céline Guérin; Jeanne and Francis La Néele
