- Confidence: high for the words. The letter does not say how Père Mazel died.
- Notes: The "missionary son" paragraph gives a rare statement, in her own hand, that her parents wished for a missionary son and hoped for one before her birth: "On m'a raconté" (someone told her). It gives no source for the story.
### F-OW-486 — "My way is all confidence and love": justice, mercy, and closing the learned book (to Roulland)
- Claim: In LT 226 she tells Roulland she does not understand why he doubts entering Heaven at once if the unbelievers killed him. She says the justice that frightens many souls "is the subject of my joy and confidence": to be just is not only to punish but to "recognise right intentions and reward virtue". She hopes as much from God's justice as from his mercy, and quotes Psalm 102 ("he knows our frailty, he remembers that we are dust"). She reasons that missionaries are "martyrs by desire and will" and that none should go to purgatory; if any trace of weakness remains, the Virgin will obtain for them "a perfect act of love". Then: "This is what I think of God's justice; my way is all confidence and love; I do not understand souls who are afraid of so tender a Friend." She writes that when she reads treatises on perfection "surrounded by a crowd of illusions", "my poor mind tires quickly, I close the learned book that breaks my head and dries my heart, and I take the Holy Scripture. Then all seems luminous... perfection seems easy: it is enough to recognise one's nothingness and abandon oneself like a child in God's arms". She adds she is glad there are "several dwellings" in God's kingdom, for she could not enter the one whose way she cannot understand.
- When / where: 9 May 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Père Adolphe Roulland (recipient)
- Source: LT 226 (to P. Roulland, 9 May 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-226-au-p-roulland-9-mai-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « ma voie est toute de confiance et d'amour, je ne comprends pas les âmes qui ont peur d'un si tendre Ami » ; « je ferme le savant livre qui me casse la tête et me dessèche le coeur et je prends l'Ecriture Sainte »
- Translation: "My way is all confidence and love; I do not understand souls that are afraid of so tender a Friend." ; "I close the learned book that breaks my head and dries my heart, and I take Holy Scripture."
- New to English readers: no (both sentences are in the 1911 edition, so in Taylor 1912); partial for the surrounding passage (the "right intentions" definition of justice and the "several dwellings" remark differ in 1911)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This is one of the fullest statements of her view of God's justice, written one month after Easter 1897. It answers Roulland's fear of purgatory; she does not describe purgatory from experience.
### F-OW-487 — Notes to Céline in Apr–May 1897: Jesus "prouder of your littleness than of millions of suns"; back-rubs and "Mr Clodion"
- Claim: LT 227 (13 May 1897): "Jesus is pleased with little Céline, to whom he gave himself for the first time 13 years ago; he is prouder of what he does in her soul, of her littleness, her poverty, than he is proud of having created millions of suns and the vastness of the heavens!" LT 228 (April–May 1897 (?)): Thérèse writes that she fears "Our Mother" will not be pleased: "she cares greatly about the frictions (rub-downs), above all on the back". "If Mr Clodion comes on Sunday to shake his long hair on my back, he will wonder why what he said was not done... Perhaps it would be better to wait until Monday. Anyway, poor one, do as you like; all will be ready tomorrow. Above all do not speak to this poor Mr; and remember that we must be rich, very rich, both of us!"
- When / where: 13 May 1897 (LT 227); April–May 1897 (LT 228), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Céline (Sr Geneviève, recipient); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress, "Notre Mère"); "Mr Clodion" (a nickname; the notes do not say for whom)
- Source: LT 227 and LT 228 (to Sr Geneviève), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-227-a-soeur-genevieve-13-mai-1897/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-228-a-soeur-genevieve-avril-mai-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « il est plus fier de ce qu'Il fait dans son âme, de sa petitesse, de sa pauvreté, qu'Il n'est fier d'avoir créé des millions de soleils et l'étendue des Cieux !... » ; « elle tient beaucoup aux frictions, surtout dans le dos »
- Translation: "He is prouder of what he does in her soul, of her littleness and poverty, than he is proud of having created millions of suns and the whole spread of the heavens!" ; "She cares a great deal about the rubbing, above all on the back."
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the words; low for "Mr Clodion" (the notes do not say who he is: a doctor, a sister, or a joke).
- Notes: The frictions on the back show a nursing care, ordered by the prioress, in spring 1897, and that Céline was involved in the arrangements (the note is an exchange between the sisters; it does not say who does the rubbing). No illness is named. "Rich, very rich" is a private phrase of the two sisters; the note does not explain it.

## Appended block E — letters LT 229–254 (23 May – 14 Jul 1897), continued numbering
### F-OW-488 — 23 May 1897: Mère Agnès is "the elevator", and "it is my little Mother who will come up"
- Claim: To Mère Agnès (23 May 1897), Thérèse fears she has hurt her: "I who would like to be her little joy, I feel on the contrary that I am her little sorrow." She says that when she is "far from this sad earth where flowers wither and birds fly away", she will be very near "my dear Mother, the Angel that Jesus sent before me to prepare the way for me, the way that leads to Heaven, the elevator that was to raise me without effort to the infinite regions of love". She adds: "it is not I who will come down, it is my little Mother who will go up to where I am." She says the good Mère Agnès did to her soul, she did to Jesus, "for he said: What you do to the least of mine you do to me. And I am the least!"
- When / where: 23 May 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (Pauline, recipient)
- Source: LT 229 (to Mère Agnès de Jésus, 23 May 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-229-a-mere-agnes-de-jesus-23-mai-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « de l'Ange que Jésus a envoyé devant moi pour me préparer le chemin, la voie qui conduit au ciel, l'ascenseur qui devait m'élever sans fatigue vers les régions infinies de l'amour » ; « ce n'est pas moi qui descendrai, c'est ma petite Mère qui montera là où je serai »
- Translation: "The Angel Jesus sent before me to prepare my way, the road that leads to Heaven, the elevator that was to lift me without effort to the infinite regions of love." ; "It is not I who will come down; it is my little Mother who will go up to where I am."
- Other sources: The "elevator" in Ms C (1897) is Jesus's arms. Here the elevator is Pauline.
- New to English readers: yes ("l'Ange que Jésus a envoyé devant moi" and "ce n'est pas moi qui descendrai" ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: It shows that in the same weeks she wrote Ms C, the "elevator" already stood for a person: Pauline. Baseline knows the elevator image only as an image for Jesus.
### F-OW-489 — 28 May 1897: she loses patience; Sr Saint-Jean-Baptiste embraces her; "Nobody, Lord"
- Claim: On the evening of 28 May 1897 Thérèse writes to Mère Agnès: "your little girl has shed sweet tears again, tears of repentance, but more of gratitude and love. This evening I showed you my virtue, my TREASURES of patience! And I who preach so well to others!" She says she is glad Mère Agnès saw her imperfection, and that Mère Agnès did not scold her: "your gentleness says more than harsh words; you are for me the image of God's mercy". "Sr St J. Baptiste, on the contrary, is usually the image of God's severity". She met her afterwards: Sr St John Baptist embraced her, saying "Poor little sister, you made me feel sorry for you, I do not want to tire you, I was wrong, etc." Thérèse says the sister spoke so because "she thinks I am going to die". Back in her cell she remembered Jesus to the adulteress ("Has no one condemned you?") and answered: "No one, Lord... neither my little Mother, image of your tenderness, nor my Sr St J. Baptist, image of your justice, and I feel I can go in peace, for you will not condemn me either." She adds: "I am much happier to have been imperfect than if, held up by grace, I had been a model of gentleness." She quotes "through the bars" (Song of Songs).
- When / where: 28 May 1897 (evening), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (recipient); Sr Saint-Jean-Baptiste (the letter says only "Sr St J.Baptiste"); Jesus and the woman taken in adultery (quoted)
- Source: LT 230 (to Mère Agnès de Jésus, 28 May 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-230-a-mere-agnes-de-jesus-28-mai-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Ah ! ce soir, je vous ai montré ma vertu, mes TRESORS de patience !... Et moi qui prêche si bien les autres !!! » ; « c'est parce qu'elle croit que je vais mourir qu'elle m'a ainsi parlé »
- Translation: "Ah! this evening I showed you my virtue, my TREASURES of patience! And I who preach so well to others!" ; "It is because she thinks I am going to die that she spoke to me like that."
- New to English readers: partial (Taylor 1912 has the 1911 text; the 1911 text hides the sister's name, see the edit card)
- Confidence: high for the words. The letter does not say what Thérèse did or said that lost her patience.
- Notes: The 1911 preface says the cause was a painting job (see the edit card). The letter does not say so. Do not state the cause from this letter.
### F-OW-490 — Edit: 1911 adds a painting job, a fever, and turns "Sr St Jean-Baptiste" into "Sœur ***"
- Claim: In the 1911 edition (Lettre VIIIe, 28 May 1897) an editorial preface says: "That day, while Sr Thérèse was suffering a strong attack of fever, one of our sisters came to ask her immediate help for a difficult painting job; for an instant her face showed the inner struggle, which Mère Agnès, who was present, noticed. That evening Thérèse wrote her this letter." The letter itself (LT 230) has no preface and no painting job. In the letter, the severe sister is named ("Sr St J.Baptiste"); in 1911 she is "Sœur ***". In 1911 she says "laissez l'ouvrage que je vous ai demandé, j'ai eu tort" ("leave the work I asked you for"), which the letter does not have (it has "je ne veux pas vous fatiguer, j'ai eu tort, etc., etc."). The 1911 text also drops the "à travers les barreaux" paragraph and the sentence "vous avez été... l'Ange chargé de me conduire", and ends on a different sentence.
- When / where: text of 28 May 1897; edition 1911
- People: Mère Agnès (editor and recipient); Sr Saint-Jean-Baptiste
- Source: LT 230 (Carmel site) compared with Histoire d'une âme 1911 (D1a-histoire-dune-ame-gutenberg-36708.txt, "Lettre VIIIe, 28 mai 1897")
- Source type: own-writing / edition
- Rights: public-domain (her words and the 1911 text); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « laissez l'ouvrage que je vous ai demandé, j'ai eu tort » (1911) against « je ne veux pas vous fatiguer, j'ai eu tort, etc., etc. » (LT 230)
- Translation: (1911) "Leave the work I asked of you; I was wrong." (LT 230) "I do not want to tire you; I was wrong, etc., etc."
- New to English readers: partial (Taylor 1912 translated the 1911 text)
- Confidence: high (both texts compared)
- Notes: The painting job and the fever in the preface come from Pauline's memory, not from the letter. The 1911 text adds a cause that the letter does not give. The sister's name was hidden in 1911.
### F-OW-491 — "The envelope" and "the letter": Mère Agnès is hurt she was not told; "do not believe what will be said to you after my death"
- Claim: On 30 May 1897 Thérèse sends two notes to Mère Agnès, who was upset that Thérèse had seemed to hide something. First note: "Do not be sad that your little girl seemed to hide something from you... if she hid a little corner of the envelope, she never hid a single line of the letter, and who knows it better than you, this little letter you love so much? To the others one can show the envelope from all sides, since they can only see that, but to you!!!" She adds: "you know now that it was on Good Friday that Jesus began to tear the envelope a little of your little letter; are you not glad he is preparing to read it, this letter you have been writing for 24 years?" In the second note ("2nd little word") she says she had put the first note in Sr Geneviève's hand as Geneviève gave her Agnès's own, regrets having posted it, and will "pay double postage" to say she understands Agnès's grief. She says she wanted, "more than you perhaps", to hide nothing but thought it better to wait. She writes: "I beg her, after my death, not to believe what may be told her." She ends: "the letter is yours; please keep writing it until the day Jesus tears the little envelope completely."
- When / where: 30 May 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (Pauline, recipient); Sr Geneviève (Céline, the go-between)
- Source: LT 231 and LT 232 (to Mère Agnès de Jésus, 30 May 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-231-a-mere-agnes-de-jesus-30-mai-1897/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-232-a-mere-agnes-de-jesus-30-mai-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « si elle a caché un petit coin de l'enveloppe, elle ne vous a jamais caché une seule ligne de la lettre » ; « c'est le Vendredi Saint que Jésus a commencé à déchirer un peu l'enveloppe » ; « je la supplie après ma mort de ne point croire ce qu'on pourra lui dire »
- Translation: "If she hid a small corner of the envelope, she never hid a single line of the letter." ; "It was on Good Friday that Jesus began to tear the envelope a little." ; "I beg her, after my death, not to believe what she may be told."
- New to English readers: yes ("déchirer un peu l'enveloppe" and "ne point croire ce qu'on pourra" ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the words. What Thérèse had "hidden" is not stated in the letters: the "envelope" seems to be her body and health (the Good Friday haemoptysis), and "the letter" her soul, but the note does not say so in plain words.
- Notes: This is the earliest recorded place where Thérèse asks her sister to distrust what others say after her death. It matters for how the community later spoke of her. The letter shows that Mère Agnès learned about the Good Friday attack later than the event, or learned the extent later ("vous savez maintenant"). Compare LT 242 and the Ms C account.
### F-OW-492 — Three tiny notes in June 1897: "too moving", "the dove", and the Wandering Jew
- Claim: LT 233 (1 Jun 1897, to Mère Agnès): "It is too moving, far too melodious! I would rather be silent than try in vain to sing what is going on in my little soul. Thank you, little Mother!" LT 237 (2 Jun 1897): "No, the little dove does not want to leave her little Mother; she wants always to fly and rest in the delightful little world of her heart. Tomorrow I will say thank you to my little Mother; I say nothing tonight so as not to break her heart and because it is too late. Baby is going to sleep." LT 239 (3 (?) Jun 1897): "I must walk until my last moment. It is he who will end my torment, like the poor Wandering Jew".
- When / where: 1 Jun, 2 Jun, 3 (?) Jun 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (recipient of all three)
- Source: LT 233, LT 237, LT 239 (to Mère Agnès de Jésus), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-233-a-mere-agnes-de-jesus-1er-juin-1897/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-237-a-mere-agnes-de-jesus-2-juin-1897/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-239-a-mere-agnes-de-jesus-3-juin-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Il faut que je marche jusqu'à mon dernier instant - C'est lui qui finira mon tourment - Comme le pauvre Juif errant – » ; « Bébé va faire dodo. »
- Translation: "I must walk until my last moment. He will end my torment, like the poor Wandering Jew." ; "Baby is going to sleep."
- New to English readers: yes ("Juif errant" ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the words; low for what "it is too moving" refers to (the note does not say).
- Notes: The "Wandering Jew" is a joke from the folk song (also LT 217). It fits the reports that she kept going about the house until the infirmary on 8 July (other cards). The text does not say what "it" or "he" is in "it is he who will end my torment" (presumably death or Jesus).
### F-OW-493 — Marie Guérin's veiling, 2 June 1897: "not a white veil but a dark veil that reminds the bride she is in exile"
- Claim: On 2 June 1897 Thérèse writes to Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (her cousin Marie Guérin) on her veil-taking. She says that on 2 June long ago "all Heaven gathered to watch" the mystery of Jesus giving himself to Marie for the first time in Communion. Now she gives herself to Jesus "before the astonished world". "It is no longer the graceful veil with long snowy folds that must wrap Marie, it is a dark veil that recalls to the bride of Jesus that she is in exile, that her Spouse is not one who will lead her to feasts, but up the mountain of Calvary. From now on Marie must look at nothing here below but the merciful God, the Jesus of the Eucharist." A second note (LT 235): "Souvenir of the beautiful day of the taking of the veil... May Thérèse's Little Jesus always caress Marie de l'Eucharistie." On 3 Jun 1897 she writes to Léonie: "How sweet it is to think that one day we will follow the Lamb together for eternity" (LT 238, "the original is at the Visitation of Caen").
- When / where: 2 Jun 1897 (LT 234, 235); 3 Jun 1897 (LT 238), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (Marie Guérin, cousin, veiled that day); Léonie Martin (recipient of LT 238)
- Source: LT 234 and LT 235 (to Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie, 2 Jun 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-234-a-soeur-marie-de-leucharistie-2-juin-1897/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-235-a-soeur-marie-de-leucharistie-2-juin-1897/ ; LT 238 (to Léonie, 3 Jun 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-238-a-leonie-3-juin-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Ce n'est plus le gracieux voile aux longs plis neigeux qui doit envelopper Marie de l'Eucharistie, c'est un sombre voile qui rappelle à l'Epouse de Jésus qu'elle est exilée »
- Translation: "It is no longer the graceful veil with long snowy folds that must wrap Marie de l'Eucharistie; it is a dark veil that reminds the Bride of Jesus that she is in exile."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: In the Carmel the professed nun's white veil is exchanged for a black one at the "prise de voile". The letter says 2 June is also the day of Marie's first visit of Jesus in Communion (« le jour de sa première visite »). The letter does not describe the ceremony itself.
### F-OW-494 — To Marie de la Trinité in June 1897: "God wants you to bear your trial alone", "stop wanting to eat earth"
- Claim: In three notes (2, 3 (?), 6 June 1897) Thérèse guides her novice Marie de la Trinité, who is in a trial. LT 236 (2 Jun): "God wants you to bear your trial alone... but my dear little p., I suffer with you!!! and I love you much." "I will come and find you a few minutes tomorrow morning, and the day after the washing I will go with you to the breads (aux pains)." LT 240 (3 (?) Jun): "Dear little flower of Jesus, I have understood everything; you need not tell me a lot: the little eye in your calyx shows me what to think of the whole little flower... But you must no longer want to eat earth; the forget-me-not must open, or rather raise its corolla, so that the Bread of Angels may come like a divine dew to strengthen it." LT 242 (6 Jun): she is glad her sadness does not make Marie melancholy. She laughs at her question about her "writings" ("what in-folios do you mean?") and says: "You will learn one day, if not on earth then in Heaven".
- When / where: 2, 3 (?), and 6 Jun 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie de la Trinité (novice, recipient)
- Source: LT 236, LT 240, LT 242 (to Sr Marie de la Trinité), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-236-a-soeur-marie-de-la-trinite-2-juin-1897/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-240-a-soeur-marie-de-la-trinite-3-juin-1897/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-242-a-soeur-marie-de-la-trinite-6-juin-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « il ne faut plus avoir envie de manger de la terre, il faut que le myosotis entrouvre ou plutôt élève sa corolle afin que le Pain des Anges vienne comme une Rosée Divine la fortifier » ; « à quels in-folio faites-vous allusion ? »
- Translation: "You must stop wanting to eat earth. The forget-me-not must open, or rather raise its corolla, so that the Bread of Angels may come like a divine dew and strengthen it." ; "What in-folios are you talking about?"
- New to English readers: yes ("manger de la terre" and "in-folio" ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the words; the trial itself is not described.
- Notes: In LT 242 (6 June) Marie de la Trinité can no longer speak to Thérèse (Thérèse says "I understand your pain at no longer being able to speak to me"), so the exchange is by letters within the house: the novice's talks were cut (by illness or by the prioress; the note does not say). "In-folios" suggests the novices already knew she was writing (Ms C). "Marie de la Trinité" is asked about "my writings" — a question whose answer is deferred to Heaven.
### F-OW-495 — 6 June 1897: "I no longer count on sickness, it is too slow a guide; I count only on love"
- Claim: Marie de la Trinité asked whether Thérèse would be glad to go to Paradise. Thérèse answers: "I would be very glad if I went, but... I do not count on sickness, it is too slow a guide. I count only on love. Ask the good Jesus that all the prayers said for me may serve to increase the Fire that is to consume me."
- When / where: 6 Jun 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie de la Trinité (recipient)
- Source: LT 242 (to Sr Marie de la Trinité, 6 Jun 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-242-a-soeur-marie-de-la-trinite-6-juin-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Vous voulez savoir si j'ai de la joie d'aller au Paradis ? J'en aurais beaucoup si j'y allais, mais... je ne compte pas sur la maladie, c'est une trop lente conductrice. Je ne compte plus que sur l'amour »
- Translation: "You want to know if I have joy at going to Paradise? I would have much if I went, but... I do not count on the illness, it is too slow a conductor. I now count only on love."
- Other sources: The 1911 edition prints "la maladie est une trop lente conductrice, je ne compte que sur l'amour" in the editors' narrative about 1896, after the Good Friday bleeding and after her cough "disappeared for some months". So the 1911 book puts the saying in spring 1896.
- New to English readers: partial (the saying is in Taylor 1912, dated to 1896; the original letter and its date, 6 June 1897, are not)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The saying "too slow a guide" now has a dated source in her own hand: 6 June 1897. The 1911 book gave it as a saying of spring 1896, which is a redating.
### F-OW-496 — 7 June 1897 to Céline: "let us run to the last place"
- Claim: To Céline, 7 June 1897: "Let us never seek what looks great in the eyes of creatures. Solomon, the wisest king ever on earth, looked at the works that occupy men under the sun, painting, sculpture, all the arts, and understood that all these things are subject to envy; he cried that they are vanity and affliction of spirit." "The only thing that is not envied is the last place; so only the last place is not vanity and affliction of spirit." She says we sometimes catch ourselves wanting what shines. Then "let us humbly rank ourselves among the imperfect, think of ourselves as little souls that God must hold up at every moment". Quoting Psalm 93: "But as soon as I said: my foot has slipped, your mercy, Lord, held me up." "It is enough to humble oneself, to bear one's imperfections gently. That is true holiness! Let us take each other by the hand, dear little sister, and run to the last place... no one will come to dispute it with us."
- When / where: 7 Jun 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Céline (Sr Geneviève, recipient, an artist who painted)
- Source: LT 243 (to Sr Geneviève, 7 Jun 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-243-a-soeur-genevieve-7-juin-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « il suffit de s'humilier, de supporter avec douceur ses imperfections. Voilà la vraie sainteté ! Prenons-nous par la main, petite soeur chérie, et courons à la dernière place... personne ne viendra nous la disputer... »
- Translation: "It is enough to humble oneself, to bear one's imperfections gently. That is true holiness! Let us take each other by the hand, dear little sister, and run to the last place. No one will come to dispute it with us."
- New to English readers: partial (the 1911 edition prints "il suffit de s'humilier... voilà la vraie sainteté" in a different setting; "courons à la dernière place" is ABSENT)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: She gives an artist (Céline) advice about painting and envy: the arts named are painting and sculpture. The letter does not give an occasion.
### F-OW-497 — 9 June 1897: her "last little word of farewell" to Bellière, "I am not dying, I am entering life"
- Claim: On 9 June 1897 Thérèse writes to Bellière while "the infirmarian is absent", "a last little word of farewell; when you receive it I will have left exile". She says she is "happy to die", "not because I will be freed from suffering here below (suffering, on the contrary, is the only thing that seems desirable to me in this valley of tears) but because I feel it is God's will." Her prioress "would like to keep me on earth"; a novena of Masses is being said "to Our Lady of Victories, who healed me in childhood, but I think the miracle she will do will be only to console the Mother who loves me so tenderly." She writes that "at the moment of appearing before God, I understand more than ever that only one thing is necessary: to work only for him and to do nothing for oneself or for creatures." She says she will go and offer his love to his friends in Heaven. She ends: "I am not dying, I am entering into life, and all I cannot tell you here below I will make you understand from the heights of Heaven. Goodbye, little Brother, pray for your little sister, who says to you: see you soon, goodbye until Heaven!"
- When / where: 9 Jun 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress, who "would like to keep me")
- Source: LT 244 (to l'abbé Bellière, 9 Jun 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-244-a-labbe-belliere-9-juin-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « je profite d'un moment où l'infirmière est absente pour vous écrire un dernier petit mot d'adieu, quand vous le recevrez j'aurai quitté l'exil » ; « je ne meurs pas, j'entre dans la vie »
- Translation: "I take advantage of a moment when the infirmarian is absent to write you a last little word of farewell; when you receive it I will have left exile." ; "I am not dying, I am entering into life."
- Other sources: The 1911 edition prints part of this text as "Lettre Xe" dated 14 Aug 1897 (see the next card). In Ms A (1911) her father asked in 1883 for a novena of Masses at Notre-Dame des Victoires; here it is a novena in June 1897.
- New to English readers: partial ("un dernier petit mot d'adieu" ABSENT from the 1911 edition; "je ne meurs pas, j'entre dans la vie" is in 1911, redated)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: She writes as though she would die in days; she lived nearly four more months. The letter mentions the infirmarian on 9 June, a month before her move to the infirmary on 8 July (she was already under a nurse's care). It records the "novena to Our Lady of Victories" for her cure.
### F-OW-498 — Edit: the 1911 edition splits and redates her farewell letters to Bellière (LT 244 and LT 253)
- Claim: The 1911 edition prints two short "letters" to Bellière as "Lettre IXe, 26 juillet 1897" and "Lettre Xe, 14 août 1897". Comparison with the Carmel text shows that (1) "Lettre IXe" ("Quand vous lirez ce petit mot, peut-être ne serai-je plus sur la terre... O mon frère, que je suis heureuse de mourir...") is the opening of LT 253, written on 13 Jul 1897, so 1911 moves it 13 days later. (2) In LT 253 she is glad to die "because I feel it is God's will and that much more than here below I will be useful to souls dear to me"; the 1911 text keeps only the second reason. (3) "Lettre Xe" ("Au moment de paraître devant le bon Dieu, je comprends plus que jamais qu'il n'y a qu'une chose nécessaire... Je ne meurs pas, j'entre dans la vie...") is the ending of LT 244, written on 9 Jun 1897, so 1911 moves it more than two months later, to 14 Aug 1897. (4) 1911 leaves out the first words of LT 244 ("I take advantage of a moment when the infirmarian is absent to write you a last little word of farewell") and the sentence about the novena to Our Lady of Victories.
- When / where: texts of 9 Jun 1897 and 13 Jul 1897; edition 1911
- People: Pauline (editor); Bellière (recipient)
- Source: LT 244 and LT 253 (Carmel site) compared with Histoire d'une âme 1911 (D1a-histoire-dune-ame-gutenberg-36708.txt, "Lettre IXe, 26 juillet 1897" and "Lettre Xe, 14 août 1897")
- Source type: own-writing / edition
- Rights: public-domain (her words and the 1911 text); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: (LT 253) « parce que je sens que telle est la volonté du bon Dieu et que bien plus qu'ici-bas, je serai utile aux âmes qui me sont chères » ; (1911) « je suis heureuse de mourir parce que, bien plus qu'ici-bas, je serai utile aux âmes qui me sont chères »
- Translation: (LT 253) "Because I feel that such is the will of the good God, and that much more than here below I will be useful to the souls dear to me." (1911) "I am happy to die because, much more than here below, I will be useful to the souls dear to me."
- New to English readers: partial (Taylor 1912 has the edited letters)
- Confidence: high (both texts read; the "Lettre IXe" text matches LT 253; the date 26 July is the date of a different letter (LT 261), and the reason for the date is not known)
- Notes: The 1911 letters put her "happy to die because useful after death" at the date 26 July and her "I am not dying, I am entering life" at 14 Aug, which makes them look like the last words of her last weeks. In fact the second is from 9 June, five months earlier than her death.
### F-OW-499 — Her own "farewell card": "Do not weep for me, for I am in Heaven", with words borrowed from Théophane Vénard
- Claim: In June (?) 1897 Thérèse made a farewell keepsake for Mère Agnès, Marie and Geneviève (her three sisters). The front top: "Do not weep for me, for I am in Heaven with the Lamb and the Sacred Virgins!" The bottom: "I see what I believed. I possess what I hoped. I am united to him whom I loved with all my power to love." The sides quote St John of the Cross: "The smallest movement of pure Love is more useful to the Church than all other works together!" On the back she copied passages from the last letters of Théophane Vénard to his family in the first person: "I find nothing on earth that makes me happy; my heart is too big..."; "I am a spring flower that the master of the garden picks for his pleasure... I, a little ephemeral one, go first!" She signs "Thérèse of the Child Jesus borrowing the thoughts of the angelic Martyr Théophane Vénard."
- When / where: June (?) 1897 per the Carmel editors (the source marks "(?)"), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus, Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, Sr Geneviève (the three sisters who received it); Théophane Vénard; St John of the Cross
- Source: LT 245 (to Agnès de Jésus, Marie du Sacré-Cœur et Geneviève, June (?) 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-245-a-agnes-de-jesus-marie-du-sacre-coeur-et-genevieve-juin-1897/
- Source type: own-writing (with copied words)
- Rights: public-domain (her words; Vénard's letters 1861 are also public-domain); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Ne pleurez pas sur moi, car je suis au Ciel avec l'Agneau et les Vierges Sacrées !... » ; « Je vois ce que j'ai cru / Je possède ce que j'ai espéré / Je suis unie à Celui que j'ai aimé / de toute ma puissance d'aimer »
- Translation: "Do not weep for me, for I am in Heaven with the Lamb and the Sacred Virgins!" ; "I see what I believed. I possess what I hoped. I am united to the One I loved with all my power to love."
- Other sources: The 1911 edition prints the Vénard passage in the narrative of Sept 1897: it says she gave it as a "souvenir d'adieu" after 6 Sept 1897, when the Carmel received a relic of Vénard. The Carmel editors here date the card to June (?) 1897. The two dates conflict.
- New to English readers: partial (the Vénard passage is in Taylor 1912, as a September event; "Ne pleurez pas sur moi" and the "Je vois ce que j'ai cru" lines are ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: medium for the date (June vs September); high for the text.
- Notes: In 1911 she tells her sisters "Théophane Vénard is a little saint; his life is quite ordinary; he loved his family a lot... I too love my family; I do not understand saints who do not love their family." She says the passages "are my thoughts; my soul is like his."
### F-OW-500 — To Sr Marthe (June 1897): "see everything in black" and "let us be so small that everyone can walk on us"
- Claim: In a note to Sr Marthe de Jésus (June (?) 1897) she writes: "do not be afraid to tell Jesus you love him, even without feeling it; it is the way to force Jesus to help you, to carry you like a child too weak to walk." "It is a great trial to see everything in black, but it does not depend entirely on you." "In Heaven you will no longer see everything in black but all in white, all clothed in the divine whiteness of our Spouse, the Lily of the valleys." She says: "Above all let us be little, so little that everyone can trample on us without our seeming to feel or suffer from it." She says she looks forward to seeing her soon.
- When / where: June (?) 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marthe de Jésus (lay sister, recipient)
- Source: LT 241 (to Sr Marthe de Jésus, June (?) 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-241-a-soeur-marthe-de-jesus-juin-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « ne craignez pas de lui dire que vous l'aimez, même sans le sentir, c'est le moyen de forcer Jésus à vous secourir » ; « soyons petites, si petites que tout le monde puisse nous fouler aux pieds »
- Translation: "Do not be afraid to tell him you love him, even without feeling it; that is the way to force Jesus to help you." ; "Let us be small, so small that everyone can trample on us."
- New to English readers: partial (the first sentences are in the 1911 edition; "foulées aux pieds" ABSENT)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The 1911 edition prints most of this note in a story about a sister in darkness. LT 80 shows Sr Marthe as her companion at her clothing (10 Jan 1889).
### F-OW-501 — 21 June 1897: Pentecost, her permission "to become an apostle in Carmel" from her father, and Bellière's first letter (15 Oct 1895)
- Claim: Thérèse tells Bellière she thanked Jesus with him for "the great grace he gave you on Pentecost day" (6 June 1897, the letter implies). She says that it was also on that feast, "10 years ago", that she got "not from my director, but from my father" permission to become an apostle in Carmel. She says that after reading "your first letter of 15 Oct 95" she thought the same as his director: "You cannot be a half saint; you must be a saint fully or not at all." She felt he must have an energetic soul and this made her glad to become his sister. She answers his shame at "wasted beautiful years": she thanks Jesus who looked on him "as he looked at the rich young man"; unlike him, Bellière followed and "left everything at the most beautiful age of life, at 18". She says she loves St Augustine and Mary Magdalene, "their repentance and above all their loving audacity". She thanks him for his dates ("I have already celebrated your 23 years") and prays for his parents "whom God has taken from this world" and "the mother you love".
- When / where: 21 Jun 1897; the Pentecost ten years earlier fell in 1887, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient); Louis Martin (father, who gave permission in 1887); St Augustine; Mary Magdalene
- Source: LT 247 (to l'abbé Bellière, 21 Jun 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-247-a-labbe-belliere-21-juin-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « c'est aussi le jour de cette belle fête (il y a 10 ans) que j'ai obtenu, non de mon directeur, mais de mon père la permission de me faire apôtre au Carmel » ; « Vous ne pourrez être un saint à demi, il vous faudra l'être tout à fait ou pas du tout »
- Translation: "It is also on that beautiful feast (10 years ago) that I obtained, not from my director but from my father, permission to become an apostle in Carmel." ; "You will not be able to be a half saint; you will have to be one fully or not at all."
- Other sources: Ms A (Pentecost 1887, the conversation with her father in the garden) is in the baseline. This letter shows she saw the day as "apostle in Carmel".
- New to English readers: yes for the framing "permission to become an apostle in Carmel", Bellière's dates (first letter 15 Oct 1895; age 23; parents dead; left at 18) and the Magdalene lines (ABSENT from the 1911 letters); partial for the passage on fear (see next card)
- Confidence: high for the words. The Carmel text says only "10 years ago" for the earlier Pentecost. Bellière's Pentecost grace is not described.
- Notes: Bellière wrote his first letter to the Carmel on 15 Oct 1895. So the correspondence began in Oct 1895; she wrote her first letter to him a year later (21 Oct 1896, LT 198), after the prioress asked her. Compare the earlier card for LT 198.
### F-OW-502 — "Love drove out all fear": she chose Carmel "to atone for the sins of others, not my own"
- Claim: In LT 247 Thérèse explains why she did not go the way of austere penance. Since she understood the love of the Heart of Jesus, "it has chased all fear from my heart. The memory of my faults humbles me... but even more it speaks to me of mercy and love." "When one throws one's faults with filial confidence into the devouring furnace of Love, how would they not be consumed without return?" She says she knows saints who spent their lives doing astonishing mortifications to atone for their sins, but "there are many dwellings in the house of the heavenly Father", and she follows the way Jesus shows her. Then: "for I did not choose an austere life to atone for my faults, but those of others". She fears she explained badly and says she does not blame Bellière's repentance or wish to atone: "now we are two, the work will go faster (and with my way I will do more work than you), and I hope one day Jesus will make you walk the same way as I do."
- When / where: 21 Jun 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient)
- Source: LT 247 (to l'abbé Bellière, 21 Jun 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-247-a-labbe-belliere-21-juin-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « je n'ai pas choisi une vie austère pour expier mes fautes, mais celles des autres » ; « Comment lorsqu'on jette ses fautes avec une confiance toute filiale dans le brasier dévorant de l'Amour, comment ne seraient-elles pas consumées sans retour ? »
- Translation: "I did not choose an austere life to atone for my own faults, but for those of others." ; "How, when one throws one's faults with filial trust into the devouring furnace of Love, could they not be burned away without return?"
- New to English readers: partial (the 1911 edition prints the "brasier" passage; the sentence "je n'ai pas choisi une vie austère pour expier mes fautes mais celles des autres" and the reference to "now we are two" are ABSENT from 1911)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The 1911 text ends the same passage with "ce que Jésus daigne opérer dans mon âme, je le lui abandonne sans réserve" and cuts the last reason, which is a plain statement of her motive for Carmel.
### F-OW-503 — Five small notes, late June – mid-July 1897: the blanket, "the branch that held the little bird", and novices in the infirmary
- Claim: LT 248 (to Léonie, late June (?) 1897): "I am more than touched by your eagerness to please me. I thank you with all my heart and am delighted with the little blanket you made me. It is just as I wanted it... I will take Communion for you tomorrow." LT 246 (13 Jun 1897, to Marie de la Trinité): a Christmas-style wish that her soul be "a dwelling perfumed with the roses of Love" and have "the burning lamp of fraternal charity that will warm his little frozen limbs". LT 249 (mid-July (?) 1897, fragments to Marie de la Trinité): "I do not want you to be sad", "the infirmary should make you understand it would be harder for you to get permission to come after Matins", "the devil goes away", and "Goodbye, poor little p., whom I must take quickly to Heaven! I want to have her whole". LT 250 (July (?) 1897, to Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph): "That gives so much good to see that He alone is perfect, that He alone must suffice for us when it pleases Him to remove the branch that held up the little bird! The bird has wings, it is made to fly!" LT 251 (Jun–Jul (?) 1897, to Sr Marthe de Jésus): "the little bride of Jesus must not be sad... Do not be a sad little girl seeing that you are not understood, judged wrongly, forgotten; but catch everyone out by trying to do for yourself what others do for you, that is, forget all that is not Jesus, forget YOURSELF." LT 252 (13 Jul 1897, to Mère Agnès): "I love you very much, my little Mamma, you will see it soon!... oh yes!"
- When / where: mid-June to mid-July 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Léonie; Sr Marie de la Trinité; Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph; Sr Marthe de Jésus; Mère Agnès de Jésus
- Source: LT 246, 248, 249, 250, 251, 252, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-246-a-soeur-marie-de-la-trinite-13-juin-1897/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-248-a-leonie-fin-juin-1897/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-249-a-soeur-marie-de-la-trinite-fragments-mi-juillet-1897/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-250-a-soeur-marie-de-saint-joseph-juillet-1897/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-251-a-soeur-marthe-de-jesus-juin-juillet-1897/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-252-a-mere-agnes-de-jesus-13-juillet-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « suis ravie de la petite couverture que tu m'as faite. Elle est telle que je la désirais » ; « lorsqu'Il Lui plaît d'ôter la branche qui soutenait le petit oiseau ! L'oiseau a des ailes, il est fait pour voler ! »
- Translation: "I am delighted with the little blanket you made me. It is just as I wished." ; "When it pleases Him to take away the branch that held up the little bird! The bird has wings; it is made to fly!"
- New to English readers: yes (all ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the words; the "infirmary" line in LT 249 is a fragment with gaps, so its sense is uncertain.
- Notes: LT 248 gives a small human detail: Léonie (the letter does not say where she lived) made a blanket for her. LT 249 mentions "the infirmary" in a fragment; the sense is not clear. LT 250: "the branch removed" for a bird is a saying about a sister's loss (she was consoled by Céline, per the note).
### F-OW-504 — 13 July 1897 to Bellière: "I will do more than write... I will be very near him" and the novena that will not cure her
- Claim: On 13 Jul 1897 Thérèse writes: "Perhaps when you read this little note I will no longer be on earth but in eternal delights! I do not know the future, but I can tell you with assurance that the Bridegroom is at the door; it would take a miracle to hold me in exile and I do not think Jesus will do this useless miracle." She is "happy to die... because I feel that such is God's will and because, much more than here below, I will be useful to the souls dear to me, yours especially". He had asked the prioress that she write often during his holiday; if the Lord prolongs her pilgrimage some weeks she will "scribble" more notes, "but most likely I will do more than write to my dear little brother, more even than speak to him the tiring language of earth; I will be very near him, I will see all that he needs, and I will give the good God no rest until he has given me all I want!" When he goes to Africa "I will follow him no longer in thought or prayer; my soul will be always with him... a little sister that Jesus gave him not to be his support for two years but until the last day of his life." She writes: "the good God has always treated me as a spoiled child; his cross has followed me since the cradle, but Jesus made me love it with passion." She asks prayers for the prioress, who "would have liked to keep me here below a long time yet" and had a novena of Masses said at Our Lady of Victories, "who had already cured me in my childhood, but I, feeling the miracle would not take place, asked and obtained from the Blessed Virgin that she console my Mother's heart, or rather make her consent that Jesus take me to Heaven".
- When / where: 13 Jul 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient; about to leave for Africa, in the letter's words "quand mon cher petit frère partira pour l'Afrique"); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress)
- Source: LT 253 (to l'abbé Bellière, 13 Jul 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-253-a-labbe-belliere-13-juillet-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « il faudrait un miracle pour me retenir dans l'exil et je ne pense pas que Jésus fasse ce miracle inutile » ; « ne laisserai pas de repos au bon Dieu qu'Il ne m'ait donné tout ce que je voudrai !... » ; « le bon Dieu m'a toujours traitée en enfant gâtée »
- Translation: "It would take a miracle to keep me in exile, and I do not think Jesus will work that useless miracle." ; "I will give the good God no rest until he has given me all I want!" ; "The good God has always treated me as a spoiled child."
- Other sources: The 1911 edition prints the first part as "Lettre IXe, 26 juillet 1897" (see the edit card above). The prioress's novena is also in LT 244.
- New to English readers: partial (the first paragraph is in 1911, redated; "je ne laisserai pas de repos au bon Dieu", the Africa promise "jusqu'au dernier jour de sa vie" and the novena passage are ABSENT from 1911)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This is four days before the 17 July saying (Carnet jaune). The letter says he is to go to Africa, so his posting was known by July 1897. It says the novena was made by the prioress.
### F-OW-505 — 14 July 1897 to Roulland: "for five or six weeks I live only on milk", and "I intend not to be idle in Heaven"
- Claim: On 14 Jul 1897 she answers Roulland's "I am a baby learning to talk": "Well, I too, for five or six weeks I have been a baby, for I live only on lolo (baby word for milk), but soon I will sit at the heavenly banquet." She says he will not have time to send his "commissions for Heaven", but she will guess them. "I intend not to stay idle in Heaven; my desire is to work still for the Church and souls; I ask it of God and I am certain he will grant it." She compares with the angels, "constantly busy with us without ever ceasing to see the divine Face". She says she will ask for him "the palm of martyrdom" and "be near you, supporting your hand so that it gathers without effort this glorious palm", and "we will fly together". She writes that "suffering has long been my Heaven here below, and I truly find it hard to imagine how I will get used to a country where joy reigns without any mixture of sadness; Jesus will have to transform my soul and give it the capacity to enjoy, otherwise I could not bear the eternal delights." She asks him to console the prioress, "whose sensitive and motherly heart has trouble consenting to my departure".
- When / where: 14 Jul 1897 (the letter does not mention her move to the infirmary on 8 Jul, which other cards record), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Père Adolphe Roulland (recipient); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress)
- Source: LT 254 (to P. Roulland, 14 Jul 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-254-au-p-roulland-14-juillet-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « depuis cinq ou six semaines, je suis aussi un bébé, car je ne vis que de lolo » ; « Je compte bien ne pas rester inactive au Ciel, mon désir est de travailler encore pour l'Eglise et les âmes » ; « depuis longtemps la souffrance est devenue mon Ciel ici-bas »
- Translation: "For five or six weeks I too have been a baby, since I live only on milk." ; "I count on not staying idle in Heaven; my desire is to go on working for the Church and for souls." ; "For a long time suffering has been my Heaven here below."
- Other sources: The 1911 edition prints the lines "ce n'est pas avec le désir égoïste de me reposer" and "depuis longtemps la souffrance est devenue mon ciel ici-bas" in its narrative. "Ce qui m'attire vers la patrie des cieux" is used as a caption in 1911.
- New to English readers: partial (the "not idle in Heaven" and "mon ciel ici-bas" lines are in 1911; "je ne vis que de lolo" and "commissions pour le Ciel" are ABSENT)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: "Five or six weeks" from 14 Jul is early June 1897: she says she has lived on milk since then. This is a dated diet detail. The letter's "I do not know how I will get used to Heaven" is a rare admission. The 17 July saying came three days later.

## Appended block F — letters LT 255–266 (16 Jul – 25 Aug 1897), continued numbering
### F-OW-506 — 16 July 1897: Communion with flowers, "a finch except when I have the fever", and "I shall not be cured but may drag on"
- Claim: To her uncle and aunt Guérin (16 Jul 1897) she writes that she is glad to show that "your little Thérèse has not yet left exile", but "with a trembling hand". She describes that morning's Communion, "made so touching, or rather triumphant, by your flowered sheaves". Her cousin Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie sang, before Communion, a verse Thérèse had composed for the morning, and, when Jesus was in her heart, the verse of "Vivre d'Amour": "To die of love is a very sweet martyrdom". Marie "had promised not to cry to please me". She says her sisters told them she is cheerful: "it is true that I am like a finch except when I have the fever; fortunately it usually comes to visit me only in the evening, at the hour when finches sleep, head hidden under the wing." She says: "One day I think I am at the door of Heaven because of the dismayed look of Mr de Cornière, and the next day he goes off joyful, saying 'You are on the way to recovery'... What I judge (I, little baby at the milk) is that I will not recover, but that I could drag on for a long time yet." She also promises to choose for her cousin Jeanne (La Néele) "the most beautiful cherub in Heaven" so that Jeanne, who lacks a child, may have "a great pontiff and a great saint".
- When / where: 16 Jul 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Isidore and Céline Guérin (uncle and aunt, recipients); Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (Marie Guérin, singer); Dr de Cornière; Jeanne and Francis La Néele (cousins)
- Source: LT 255 (to M. et Mme Guérin, 16 Jul 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-255-a-m-et-mme-guerin-16-juillet-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « je suis comme un pinson excepté quand j'ai la fièvre ; heureusement elle ne vient ordinairement me visiter que le soir » ; « Ce que je juge, moi (petit bébé au lolo), c'est que je ne guérirai pas mais que je pourrais traîner longtemps encore »
- Translation: "I am like a finch, except when I have the fever; fortunately it usually visits me only in the evening." ; "What I judge, I (little baby on milk), is that I will not be cured but that I could drag on a long time yet."
- New to English readers: yes ("de Cornière", "pinson excepté quand j'ai la fièvre" and "traîner longtemps encore" ABSENT from the 1911 letters)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This gives her own view of the doctor's shifts (a day of alarm, a day of hope) and her own prognosis. It says the fever comes in the evening (LT 222 bis of April said "3 o'clock"). The letter does not say who wrote the verse Marie sang first (Thérèse composed it "for this morning").
### F-OW-507 — 17 July 1897 letter to Léonie, written the same day as the "spend my heaven doing good" saying
- Claim: On 17 Jul 1897, Thérèse writes to Léonie: "I am very happy to be able to talk with you still; a few days ago I no longer thought I would have this consolation on earth, but the good God seems to want to prolong my exile a little. I am not sad about it, for I would not enter Heaven one minute sooner by my own will." She says: "The only happiness on earth is to apply oneself to find delightful always the part that Jesus gives us." She tells Léonie "the world is nothing to you", so she can be a saint. She promises: "You want me in Heaven to pray the Sacred Heart for you; be sure I will not forget to make your commissions to him and to claim all you need to become a great saint." She hopes that thinking of her entry into Heaven fills Léonie with joy, "since I will be able to love you still more". She adds a note: "I will write you at more length another time; I cannot now, baby needing to go to sleep."
- When / where: 17 Jul 1897, Carmel of Lisieux (infirmary)
- People: Léonie Martin (recipient)
- Source: LT 257 (to Léonie, 17 Jul 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-257-a-leonie-17-juillet-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « je ne voudrais point entrer au Ciel une minute plus tôt par ma propre volonté » ; « je pourrai t'aimer encore davantage »
- Translation: "I would not enter Heaven one minute sooner by my own will." ; "I will be able to love you even more."
- Other sources: The Carnet jaune of 17 July 1897 records her saying "I want to spend my Heaven doing good on earth" (see the Carnet jaune cards to come).
- New to English readers: partial (the passage on "une minute plus tôt" is in the 1911 edition; the closing lines about "baby" and the Sacred Heart commissions are ABSENT)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: It is a rare example where one day of her life is documented twice, once in her own letter and once in the Carnet jaune. In the letter she talks of Heaven as a place from which she can "love more" and "make commissions", not of doing good on earth.
### F-OW-508 — 18 July 1897 to Bellière: "the thought of heavenly happiness gives me no joy", she never asked to die young
- Claim: Bellière wrote that she should not tell him of her joy at approaching happiness. Thérèse writes: "If for a few moments you could read my soul, how surprised you would be! The thought of heavenly happiness, far from causing me any joy, makes me wonder sometimes how it will be possible for me to be happy without suffering. Jesus will surely change my nature; otherwise I would miss suffering and the valley of tears." "I never asked God to die young; that would have seemed cowardice to me; but from childhood he gave me the inner conviction that my course here below would be short. So the only thing that makes me joyful is the thought of doing the Lord's will." She then quotes Jesus at the Last Supper ("I go to my Father... it is to your advantage that I go") and promises: "When I am in port I will teach you how to sail on the stormy sea of the world, with the abandon and love of a child who knows his Father loves him and will not leave him alone in danger." She tells him "you are called to rise to God by the elevator of love and not to climb the rough stairs of fear", and says "soon, like St Augustine, you will say: love is the weight that draws me".
- When / where: 18 Jul 1897, Carmel of Lisieux (infirmary)
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient); St Augustine (quoted)

- Original words: « La pensée du bonheur céleste, non seulement ne me cause aucune joie, mais encore je me demande parfois comment il me sera possible d'être heureuse sans souffrir » ; « Jamais je n'ai demandé au bon Dieu de mourir jeune, cela m'aurait paru de la lâcheté »
- Translation: "The thought of heavenly happiness not only causes me no joy, but I sometimes wonder how I will be able to be happy without suffering." ; "I never asked the good God to die young; that would have seemed like cowardice to me."

### F-OW-509 — The two children and the father: the parable in the 18 July letter (the one who asks to be punished with a kiss)
- Claim: To help Bellière trust Jesus, Thérèse tells this story: "I suppose a father has two mischievous and disobedient children. Coming to punish them, he sees one tremble and move away in terror, though he feels in his heart that he deserves punishment. His brother, on the contrary, throws himself into his father's arms, saying he regrets having hurt him, that he loves him, and that to prove it he will be good from now on. Then the child asks his father to punish him with a kiss. I do not think the happy father's heart could resist the filial confidence of his child, whose sincerity and love he knows. He knows that more than once his son will fall again into the same faults, but he is disposed to forgive him always, if always his son takes him by the heart... I say nothing of the first child, dear little brother; you must understand whether his father can love him as much and treat him with the same indulgence as the other."
- When / where: 18 Jul 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient)
- Source: LT 258 (to l'abbé Bellière, 18 Jul 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-258-a-labbe-belliere-18-juillet-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « je suppose qu'un père ait deux enfants espiègles et désobéissants... cet enfant demande à son père de le punir par un baiser »
- Translation: "I suppose a father has two mischievous and disobedient children... this child asks his father to punish him with a kiss."
- Other sources: LT 191 (12 Jul 1896) tells a one-child version (the child who says "Kiss me, I will not do it again") and is in the 1911 edition.
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The two-children version makes a contrast between the fearful child and the trusting child. The parable is in her own words, not in Story of a Soul. It is among the clearest short statements of the "little way" for a priest.
### F-OW-510 — The photograph "in view of my death" and Mère Agnès who "speaks of my death like a feast"
- Claim: In the same letter (LT 258) Thérèse tells Bellière that Mère Agnès (Pauline) "is twice my sister and served as my mother in my childhood". "Our good Mother" feared Agnès's sensitive nature would make her departure bitter. "The contrary happened: she speaks of my death as a feast, and that is a great consolation to me." She asks him to try, like Agnès, to be persuaded that "instead of losing me you will find me, and that I will not leave you any more". She cannot give him what he asks (he wanted a photograph) "because I have made the vow of poverty; I cannot even dispose of a holy card; only our Mother can satisfy you". She adds: "Just now, in view of my approaching death, a sister photographed me for our Mother's feast. The novices cried out when they saw that I had put on my 'grand air'; it seems I am usually smiling more; but believe, little brother, that if my photograph does not smile at you, my soul will not stop smiling at you when it is near you."
- When / where: 18 Jul 1897 (the photograph was taken before, for the prioress's feast), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient); Mère Agnès de Jésus (Pauline); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress); a sister who took the photograph (the letter does not name her); the novices
- Source: LT 258 (to l'abbé Bellière, 18 Jul 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-258-a-labbe-belliere-18-juillet-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « elle parle de ma mort comme d'une fête et c'est une grande consolation pour moi » ; « en vue de ma mort prochaine, une soeur m'a photographiée pour la fête de notre Mère. Les novices se sont écriées en me voyant que j'avais pris mon grand air »
- Translation: "She speaks of my death as of a feast, and that is a great consolation to me." ; "In view of my approaching death, a sister photographed me for our Mother's feast. The novices cried out when they saw me that I had put on my 'grand air'."
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)

### F-OW-511 — 22 July 1897 (Mary Magdalene's day): "Let the just man strike me"; and small notes on 16 and 24 July
- Claim: On 22 Jul 1897 (the feast of St Mary Magdalene) she copies for Céline a verse from Psalm 140 as a prayer: "Let the just man strike me out of compassion for sinners; let the oil with which one perfumes the head not soften mine." She explains: "I can be broken and tried only by the just, since all my sisters are pleasing to God. It is less bitter to be broken by a sinner than by a just man, but out of compassion for sinners, to obtain their conversion, I ask you, my God, to be broken for them by the just souls around me." She also asks that "the oil of praise, so sweet to nature, not soften my head, that is my mind, by making me think I have virtues I have hardly practised several times". LT 256 (16 (?) Jul 1897): to Sr Marthe, "your little twin", she says she forgot her birthday and will give her "the prayer on humility, not yet quite finished being copied". LT 260 (24–25 (?) Jul 1897): to the Guérins, she sends flowers (thoughts for her uncle and aunt, two rose buds for Jeanne and Francis, a lone bud for Léonie) with "the fruits of the Holy Spirit, particularly that of Joy".

- People: Céline (Sr Geneviève); Sr Marthe de Jésus; the Guérins and Jeanne, Francis and Léonie

- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Que le juste me brise par compassion pour les pécheurs, que l'huile dont on parfume la tête n'amollisse pas la mienne. » ; « Votre petite jumelle qui ne pourrait dormir si elle ne vous envoyait ce petit mot. »
- Translation: "Let the just man break me out of compassion for sinners; let the oil with which the head is perfumed not soften mine." ; "Your little twin, who could not sleep if she did not send you this little note."
- Other sources: The 1911 edition prints the Psalm-140 note in Céline's testimony ("I received from her this note in a moment of temptation and combat").

### F-OW-512 — 26 July 1897 to Bellière: "the onions of Egypt" and "the hidden manna"; no need to "put my hand on Jesus's mouth"
- Claim: Bellière wrote that he is resigned to losing her "presence, sensible action". Thérèse promises: "after my departure for eternal life I will let you taste what happiness there is in feeling a friendly soul near you. It will not be this more or less distant correspondence, always incomplete, that you seem to regret, but a fraternal conversation that will charm the angels, a conversation creatures cannot blame since it will be hidden from them." She jokes: "How good it will seem to be freed from this mortal shell, which, if by impossible I found myself with several people in the presence of my dear little brother, would oblige me to look at him as a stranger, an indifferent!" She tells him not to imitate the Hebrews who "regretted the onions of Egypt" ("I have served you those vegetables that make you cry when you bring them near your eyes uncooked"); she will share "the hidden manna". He had feared that a detailed account of his faults could lessen her affection. She answers that she will not need "to put my hand on Jesus's mouth"; he "has long since forgotten your unfaithfulnesses"; and she tells him to follow "the first impulse that draws you into his arms". She then says that Jesus is more saddened by the many small "indelicacies" of his friends than by the grave faults of people of the world, but says that only when the friends make a habit of them and do not ask forgiveness does Jesus say "These wounds I received in the house of those who loved me" (a text of Holy Week).
- When / where: 26 Jul 1897, Carmel of Lisieux (infirmary)
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient)
- Source: LT 261 (to l'abbé Bellière, 26 Jul 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-261-a-labbe-belliere-26-juillet-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « un entretien fraternel qui charmera les anges, un entretien que les créatures ne pourront blâmer puisqu'il leur sera caché » ; « je n'aurai pas besoin de «mettre la main sur la bouche de Jésus» ! Il a depuis longtemps oublié vos infidélités »
- Translation: "A fraternal conversation that will charm the angels, a conversation creatures cannot blame because it will be hidden from them." ; "I will not need to 'put my hand over Jesus's mouth'! He forgot your unfaithfulnesses long ago."
- Other sources: The "wounds in the house of those who loved me" passage is in the 1911 edition, in another letter of hers (see the LT 190 card).
- New to English readers: yes ("oignons d'Egypte" and "main sur la bouche de Jésus" ABSENT from the 1911 edition; the wounds passage is known)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The letter says Bellière called his sin-list "an indiscretion" and that she had "served" him "onions" (her earlier severe letters). It shows her reading of her role as "sister" in Heaven: a hidden conversation, unlike letters read by the prioress.
### F-OW-513 — Her own account of her family to Bellière (26 Jul 1897): four little angels, "three times up the mountain of Carmel"
- Claim: Bellière asked whether in the world his "sister" was called Geneviève (he thought she was Céline). Thérèse answers that he was not well informed and gives "details on my family". "The good God gave me a father and a mother more worthy of Heaven than of earth. They asked the Lord to give them many children and take them for himself. This desire was granted: four little angels flew to Heaven, and the 5 children left in the arena took Jesus as spouse." Her father "like a new Abraham, climbed three times the mountain of Carmel to sacrifice to God what he had dearest". First his two elders; then "the third of his daughters, on the advice of her director and led by our incomparable father, tried a convent of the Visitation" (she came back and "lives in the world as in the cloister"). "Only two children were left, one aged 18, the other 14." The younger, "little Thérèse", asked to fly to Carmel, which "she obtained without difficulty from her good Father, who went as far as taking her first to Bayeux, then to Rome to remove the obstacles that delayed the immolation of her whom he called his queen". At the port he said to the last child: "If you want to follow your sisters' example, I consent; do not worry about me." She says the "angel who was to support the old age of such a saint" (Céline) answered that after his departure to Heaven she would fly to the cloister. She signs "not Geneviève but Thérèse of the Child Jesus of the Holy Face".
- When / where: 26 Jul 1897 (about events of 1860s–1894), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient); Louis and Zélie Martin (parents); Marie, Pauline, Léonie, Céline, Thérèse (the five daughters); four children who died young
- Source: LT 261 (to l'abbé Bellière, 26 Jul 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-261-a-labbe-belliere-26-juillet-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « ils demandèrent au Seigneur de leur donner beaucoup d'enfants et de les prendre pour Lui. Ce désir fut exaucé, quatre petits anges s'envolèrent aux Cieux, et les 5 enfants restées dans l'arène prirent Jésus pour époux » ; « mon père, comme un nouvel Abraham, gravit trois fois la montagne du Carmel »
- Translation: "They asked the Lord to give them many children and take them for himself. This wish was granted: four little angels flew to Heaven, and the five children left in the arena took Jesus as spouse." ; "My father, like a new Abraham, climbed the mountain of Carmel three times."
- New to English readers: yes ("quatre petits anges... 5 enfants restées dans l'arène" as a family summary, "gravit trois fois la montagne du Carmel" and "Si tu veux suivre l'exemple de tes soeurs, j'y consens" ABSENT from the 1911 letters)
- Confidence: high for the words; the summary is a pious retelling in her last summer, not a neutral record.
- Notes: The letter is her own family history in one page, for a stranger. It says the third daughter (Léonie) "tried" the Visitation and "later came back to the world where she lives as if in the cloister" (other cards record that Léonie left the Visitation in July 1895). The letter says the two children left were aged 18 and 14, and Thérèse is the one who asked.
### F-OW-514 — Her father's illness, told to Bellière: "paralysis in the legs, then in the head", three years away, "he did not want his healing asked for"
- Claim: In the same letter she says a short time after she left home the father "was seized by an attack of paralysis in the legs, which returned several times, but it could not settle there, the trial would have been too sweet, for the heroic patriarch had offered himself to God as a victim; so the paralysis, changing its course, settled in the venerable head of the victim whom the Lord had accepted". She says "we had to drink the chalice to the dregs and be separated for three years from our venerated father, entrusting him to religious but foreign hands". "He accepted this trial of which he understood all the humiliation, and carried heroism to the point of not wanting his healing to be asked for." She apologises: "I hope to write you again if the trembling of my hand does not increase, for I had to write my letter in several sittings."
- When / where: told 26 Jul 1897; events 1888–1892
- People: Louis Martin (father); Abbé Bellière (recipient)
- Source: LT 261 (to l'abbé Bellière, 26 Jul 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-261-a-labbe-belliere-26-juillet-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « l'héroïque patriarche s'était offert à Dieu en victime, aussi la paralysie changeant son cours se fixa dans la tête vénérable de la victime que le Seigneur avait acceptée... » ; « poussa l'héroïsme jusqu'à ne pas vouloir qu'on demandât sa guérison »
- Translation: "The paralysis, changing its course, settled in the venerable head of the victim whom the Lord had accepted." ; "He carried heroism to the point of not wanting his healing to be asked for."
- Other sources: Ms A names "paralysis" in general terms; see cards on Caen (1889–1892) and on his death (29 Jul 1894).
- New to English readers: partial (the details "paralysis in the legs, then head" and "he did not want his healing asked for" are ABSENT from the 1911 letters; Taylor 1912 tells the illness from Ms A)
- Confidence: high for her words; this is her interpretation of his illness, not a medical record.
- Notes: She says three years apart (1889–1892); other cards give the dates of the Bon-Sauveur stay. She writes "the trembling of my hand" and that she had to write in several sittings.
### F-OW-515 — 3 Aug 1897: "the valley of the shadow of death... you are with me"
- Claim: On 3 Aug 1897 she writes for Céline (Sr Geneviève) a short prayer: "O my God, how sweet you are to the little victim of your Merciful Love! Now that you join outward suffering to the trials of my soul, I cannot say 'the anguish of death has surrounded me' but I cry in my gratitude: 'I have gone down into the valley of the shadow of death, yet I fear no evil: because you are with me, Lord!'" The note is dated "3 August 1897 - Ps XXII. 4".

### F-OW-516 — 10 Aug 1897 to Bellière: "my passport for Heaven", 29 July, and extreme unction on 30 July
- Claim: On 10 Aug 1897 Thérèse writes: "I am now quite ready to leave; I have received my passport for Heaven, and it is my dear father who obtained this grace for me: on the 29th he gave me the guarantee that I would soon join him." (29 July is the date of her father's death in 1894; the letter does not say so.) "The next day, the doctor, astonished at the progress the illness had made in two days, told our good Mother that it was time to fulfil my desires by having me receive Extreme Unction. I had this joy on the 30th, and also the joy of seeing Jesus-Host leave the tabernacle for me, whom I received as Viaticum of my long journey!... This Bread of Heaven strengthened me; see, my pilgrimage seems unable to end. Far from complaining, I am glad the good God lets me suffer still for love of him; how sweet it is to abandon oneself in his arms, without fears or desires."
- When / where: 29–30 Jul 1897; told 10 Aug 1897, Carmel of Lisieux (infirmary)
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient); Louis Martin (father, "mon père chéri"); the doctor (unnamed here); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress)
- Source: LT 263 (to l'abbé Bellière, 10 Aug 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-263-a-labbe-belliere-10-aout-1897/

- Translation: "I have received my passport for Heaven, and my dear father obtained this grace for me: on the 29th he gave me the guarantee that I will soon join him." ; "The doctor, astonished at the progress the illness had made in two days, told our good Mother that it was time to fulfil my desires by having me receive Extreme Unction."

### F-OW-517 — Bellière's "inheritance": a reliquary, a worn crucifix, and the last picture she painted (10 Aug 1897)
- Claim: In LT 263 she lists what the prioress will give Bellière after her death: "1° The reliquary I received the day of my clothing, which has never left me since. 2) A little Crucifix that is incomparably dearer to me than the big one, for it is no longer the first that was given to me that I have now. In Carmel one sometimes changes objects of piety, a good way to keep one from getting attached. I come back to the little Crucifix. It is not beautiful, the figure of Christ has almost disappeared; you will not be surprised when you know that since I was 13 this souvenir from one of my sisters has followed me everywhere. It was above all during my journey in Italy that this Crucifix became precious to me; I had it touch all the important relics I had the happiness to venerate; to say the number would be impossible; moreover it was blessed by the Holy Father. Since I have been ill I hold our dear little Crucifix almost always in my hands; looking at it I think with joy that after receiving my kisses it will go to claim those of my little brother." She adds that the prioress will also give him "the last picture I painted". She thanks him for his photograph.
- When / where: 10 Aug 1897, Carmel of Lisieux (infirmary)
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress, who will give); one of her sisters (donor of the crucifix, at age 13)
- Source: LT 263 (to l'abbé Bellière, 10 Aug 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-263-a-labbe-belliere-10-aout-1897/
- Source type: own-writing

- Translation: "It is not beautiful; the figure of Christ has almost disappeared... since I was 13 this keepsake from one of my sisters has followed me everywhere." ; "Since I have been ill I hold our dear little Crucifix almost always in my hands."

### F-OW-518 — The Blessed "are more merciful, not more strict": her view of Heaven, and Bellière's new "dignity"
- Claim: In LT 263 she disagrees with Bellière's idea of Heaven: he thinks that sharing in God's justice and holiness she will not be able to excuse his faults as on earth. She answers: "Have you forgotten that I will also share in the infinite mercy of the Lord? I believe the Blessed have great compassion for our miseries, they remember that, being like us fragile and mortal, they committed the same faults and fought the same battles, and their brotherly tenderness becomes even greater than it was on earth; that is why they never cease to protect us and pray for us." A postscript: "I congratulate you on your new dignity; on the 25th, the day I keep the feast of my dear little father, I will have the happiness to keep the feast of my brother Louis de France too." She also writes: "(It is by choice that I became your sister.)"

- Source type: own-writing
