### F-OW-562 — 31 July 1897: "I found happiness and joy on earth, but only in suffering" — the spark under the ashes
- Claim: On 31 July she said: "I have found happiness and joy on earth, but only in suffering, for I have suffered much here below; this must be made known to souls..." She explained: "Since my first Communion, since I asked Jesus to change for me into bitterness all the consolations of the earth, I had a perpetual desire to suffer. Yet I did not think of making it my joy; that is a grace given me only later. Until then it was like a spark hidden under ashes, and like the flowers of a tree that must become fruits in their time. But seeing my flowers always fall, that is, letting myself go to tears when I suffered, I said to myself with surprise and sadness: but it will never be more than desires!"
- When / where: 31 Jul 1897, infirmary
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 31.7.13, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-juillet-1897/
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « J'ai trouvé le bonheur et la joie sur la terre, mais uniquement dans la souffrance, car j'ai beaucoup souffert ici-bas ; il faudra le faire savoir aux âmes... » ; « c'était comme une étincelle cachée sous la cendre, et comme les fleurs d'un arbre qui doivent devenir des fruits en leur temps »
- Translation: "I have found happiness and joy on earth, but only in suffering, for I have suffered much here below; it must be made known to souls..." ; "It was like a spark hidden under the ashes, and like the flowers of a tree that must become fruit in their time."
- New to English readers: yes (keyword check: "found happiness" no match in Novissima Verba)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: She asks that this be made known: a dated instruction to the recorder. The saying gives the timeline of her attitude to suffering: desire since first Communion (1884); joy in it a later grace.
### F-OW-563 — 31 July 1897: the doctor says "a month or more" the day after saying "today": "It leaves me in deep calm"; "the good God has deceived everyone"
- Claim: On 31 July she said: "This evening, when you told me that M. de Cornière thought I had a month or more, I could not believe it; it was such a great difference from yesterday when he said they had to administer me that very day! But it left me in deep calm. What does it matter to me to remain a long time on earth! If I suffer much and ever more, I have no fear; the good God will give me strength; he will not abandon me." A sister said "If you live a long time, nobody will understand anything!" She answered: "What does it matter! Everybody can well despise me; that is what I always wanted; I will have it at the end of my life!" And: "Now that the good God has done what he wanted to do, that he has deceived everybody... He will come like a thief at the hour when one no longer thinks of it; that is my little idea."
- When / where: 31 Jul 1897, infirmary
- People: Dr de Cornière; Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 31.7.14–16, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-juillet-1897/
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Qu'est-ce que cela me fait de rester encore longtemps sur la terre ! Si je souffre beaucoup et toujours davantage, je n'ai point peur, le bon Dieu me donnera la force, il ne m'abandonnera pas. » ; « Tout le monde peut bien me mépriser, c'est toujours ce que j'ai désiré ; je l'aurai à la fin de ma vie ! »
- Translation: "What does it matter to me to stay a long time on earth! If I suffer much and ever more, I am not afraid; the good God will give me strength; he will not abandon me." ; "Everyone may well despise me; it is what I always wanted; I will have it at the end of my life!"
- Other sources: LT 263 (10 Aug 1897) gives the doctor's view of 30 July from her side.
- New to English readers: partial ("month" appears in Novissima Verba by keyword; not compared)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: These lines give the doctor's swing in two days: "administer today" (30 July) and "a month or more" (31 July). Her prediction "if you live a long time nobody will understand" is a remark by a sister.

## Appended block J — poems, plays, prayers, and the Diana Vaughan link (dates checked on the Carmel pages), continued numbering
### F-OW-575 — PN 45 "My joy" (21 Jan 1897): "All my soul is there!"; and the scratched-out line in LT 216
- Claim: The Carmel editors say PN 45 "Ma joie" was written at Mère Agnès's request for her feast on 21 Jan 1897, and that Thérèse said as she gave it: "All my soul is there!" It has seven stanzas (tune "Rêve, parfum ou frais murmure"). Every stanza except the last begins "Ma joie, c'est..." ("My joy is to remain little", "to hide myself, to lower myself", "to love suffering"). Last stanza: "Long yet I am willing to live, Lord, if that is your wish; in Heaven I would like to follow you if that pleased you... What are death or life to me? Jesus, my joy is to love you!" The editors also say that in the original of LT 216 (9 Jan 1897) the second half of the phrase "since if there is a Heaven, it is for me" was scratched out, after the copy of her writings made for the Process in 1910, probably because the recipient thought it improper to write a doubt of Heaven; they note the letter's theme continues in PN 45.
- When / where: 21 Jan 1897 (poem); 9 Jan 1897 (letter)
- People: Mère Agnès (recipient of both); Thérèse
- Source: PN 45, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/pn-45/ ; Carmel editors on LT 216 (in _work/lw_all.txt, line 15)
- Source type: own-writing (context and the scratching: Carmel editors, facts only)
- Rights: public-domain (her words); editors' notes: unknown
- Original words: « Ma joie, c'est de rester petite » ; « Que me font la mort ou la vie? / Jésus, ma joie, c'est de t'aimer ! »
- Translation: "My joy is to stay little." ; "What do death or life matter to me? Jesus, my joy is to love you!"
- New to English readers: yes for "Toute mon âme est là" and the scratching; partial for the poem (in Taylor 1912)
- Confidence: medium (the scratching is the editors' report of the manuscript)
- Notes: The editors say the second half of the LT 216 phrase was erased from the original letter after the 1910 copy. Compare our card on LT 216. Who scratched it is the editors' inference ("the recipient").
### F-OW-576 — PN 47 "To Théophane Vénard" (2 Feb 1897): a poem to the saint on the day of his death
- Claim: The Carmel page dates PN 47 to 2 Feb 1897, the anniversary of Vénard's death (the page's sub-title says he was martyred in Tonkin at 31), and says it is offered by Thérèse to "her much-loved saint". The sub-title: "Priest of the Foreign Missions, martyred in Tonkin at the age of 31 years". Tune "Les adieux du Martyr". Seven stanzas. Stanza 6: "I too love that unfaithful shore that was the object of your ardent love; with happiness I would fly to it if the good God called me there one day." Stanza 7 asks him to come at her last hour: "If I were a spring flower that the Lord would soon gather, come down from Heaven at my last hour, I beg you, O Blessed Martyr!"
- When / where: 2 Feb 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Théophane Vénard; Thérèse
- Source: PN 47, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/pn-47/
- Source type: own-writing (date and dedication: Carmel editors)
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je l'aime aussi, cette plage infidèle / Qui fut l'objet de ton ardent amour / Avec bonheur, je volerais vers elle / Si le Bon Dieu m'y appelait un jour... » ; « Descends du Ciel à mon heure dernière »
- Translation: "I love that unbelieving shore too, which was the object of your ardent love; with happiness I would fly there if the good God called me one day." ; "Come down from Heaven at my last hour."
- Other sources: LT 221 (19 Mar 1897) explains the "Tonkin" stanza to Roulland; the Carnet jaune has the relics of Vénard pinned to her bed curtain; CJ 25.7 says the picture of Vénard was near her.
- New to English readers: partial (Taylor 1912 has the poem; the date and its place in her last year are not in the baseline)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The Carmel says the day is the anniversary of his death; the year 1861 is outside knowledge, not on the page. Her identification with him: she copied words of his letters in her farewell keepsake (see the LT 245 card).
### F-OW-577 — RP 8, "Saint Stanislas Kostka" (8 Feb 1897): a play for Sr Saint-Stanislas's golden jubilee
- Claim: The Carmel page dates RP 8 to 8 Feb 1897, the golden jubilee (50 years of profession) of Sr Saint-Stanislas. The cast: the Virgin and the Child Jesus; St Stanislas Kostka; St Francis Borgia, duke of Gandía, "lord of the court of Charles V", General of the Jesuits; and Brother Stephen Augusti, a young novice. Scene 1: in Rome, in St Francis Borgia's room; the novice kneels and says he cannot speak to the general otherwise than on his knees. Tunes: "L'Ange et l'âme" and "Le Soir".
- When / where: 8 Feb 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Saint-Stanislas (jubilarian); St Stanislas Kostka; St Francis Borgia; Thérèse (author)
- Source: RP 8, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/rp-8-saint-stanislas-kostka/
- Source type: own-writing (date and occasion: Carmel editors)
- Rights: public-domain (her text); Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « Mon Révérend Père, je suis confus de votre bonté ; comment pouvez-vous me traiter en ami, vous, le général de la compagnie de Jésus »
- Translation: "Reverend Father, I am confused by your kindness; how can you treat me as a friend, you, the general of the Society of Jesus?"
- New to English readers: yes (not in the baseline)
- Confidence: high for the date and cast
- Notes: Her last play, written in Feb 1897 when she was already ill. PS 3 "Depuis cinquante ans" (a poem for the same sister's anniversary of 1845/1846) is a second piece for this sister. The saint is a link to another young saint (Stanislas Kostka, a Jesuit novice who died at 17).
### F-OW-579 — PN 51 "A rose stripped of its petals" (19 May 1897) and PN 50 "To Joan of Arc" (May? 1897)
- Claim: PN 51 has five stanzas dated 19 May 1897; it was written at the request of Mère Henriette of the Carmel of Paris (Avenue de Messine). Image: Jesus, taking his first steps from his Mother's arms, and a rose stripped of its petals laid under his feet: "A stripped rose is simply thrown to the wind... like her I abandon myself to you, little Jesus." The editors say Mère Henriette, through Marie de la Trinité, asked for a last stanza on the reward in Heaven; Thérèse answered: "Let the good Mother make that verse herself." PN 50 "To Joan of Arc" (5 stanzas, "May ? 1897", for herself): the editors say she wrote it spontaneously, very ill, rereading her plays, on the theme of prison. It says Joan appears "brighter and more beautiful" in her dark prison than at the king's coronation, and that betrayal brought her the heavenly glow.
- When / where: 19 May 1897 (PN 51); May (?) 1897 (PN 50)
- People: Mère Henriette (Carmel of Paris, avenue de Messine); Marie de la Trinité (go-between); Joan of Arc; Thérèse
- Source: PN 51, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/pn-51/ ; PN 50, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/pn-50/
- Source type: own-writing (dates and context: Carmel editors)
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « Une rose effeuillée sans recherche se donne / Pour n'être plus. » ; « Jeanne tu m'apparais plus brillante et plus belle / Qu'au sacre de ton roi, dans ta sombre prison. »
- Translation: "A stripped rose gives itself without design, so as to be no more." ; "Joan, you appear to me brighter and more beautiful in your dark prison than at the crowning of your king."
- New to English readers: partial (PN 51 is well known; the request from Paris and the reply about the last stanza are not; PN 50 and its context are new)
- Confidence: high for the texts; medium for the editors' dating of PN 50 ("mai ?").
- Notes: The "stripped rose" is the image behind the "shower of roses" saying. PN 50 shows her final view of Joan: not the victorious knight but the prisoner.
### F-OW-580 — PN 54 "Why I love you, O Mary" (May 1897): the poem on all she would preach on the Virgin
- Claim: The Carmel editors date PN 54 to May 1897 and say Thérèse wrote it for herself, encouraged by Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur. They quote her words to Céline: "I have always dreamed of expressing in a song to the Blessed Virgin all that I think of her" (given as "PO 667", the process). The page gives the tune: "Pourquoi m'avoir livré l'autre jour, ô ma Mère". It is her longest poem in her last year (the text file is about 10,000 characters). On 21 Aug 1897 she said of the poem: "I have said in my Canticle 'Why I love you, O Mary!' all that I would preach about her" (CJ 21.8.3).
- When / where: May 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (who encouraged her); Céline (told); Thérèse
- Source: PN 54, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/pn-54/ ; CJ 21.8.3 (Paroles retrouvées, 21 Aug)
- Source type: own-writing (date and context: Carmel editors)
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « Enfin j'ai dit dans mon Cantique : «Pourquoi je t'aime, ô Marie!» tout ce que je prêcherais sur elle. »
- Translation: "In the end I said in my Canticle 'Why I love you, O Mary!' everything I would preach about her."
- Other sources: The Carnet jaune, 21 Aug 1897, records her longer talk on how she would preach on the Virgin (existing card).
- New to English readers: partial (Taylor 1912 has the poem)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The poem date "May 1897" is the editors' date; the exact day is not given.
### F-OW-581 — PS 8, "You who know my extreme littleness": her last poem, composed in a sleepless night
- Claim: The Carmel editors date PS 8 to "a few days before 16 July 1897", for herself. It has one stanza (8 lines): "You who know my extreme littleness, you do not fear to lower yourself to me! Come into my heart, O white Host I love; come into my heart, it longs for you! Ah! I would like your goodness to let me die of love after this favour. Jesus! hear the cry of my tenderness. Come into my heart!" The Carnet jaune (13 Jul 1897, no. 4) says she composed the verse "during the night" for the Communion: "I composed it very easily, it is extraordinary; I thought I could no longer make verses." On the feast of Our Lady of Mount Carmel, 16 Jul 1897, Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie sang it before her Communion in the infirmary, and after Communion the verse of "Vivre d'amour" (stanza 14) (Carmel editors; LT 255).
- When / where: about 13 Jul 1897 (composed); 16 Jul 1897 (sung), infirmary
- People: Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (Marie Guérin, singer); Thérèse; Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: PS 8, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/ps8/ ; CJ 13.7.4, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-juillet-1897/ ; LT 255 (16 Jul 1897)
- Source type: own-writing; eyewitness record (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « Toi qui connais ma petitesse extrême / Tu ne crains pas de t'abaisser vers moi ! » ; « Je l'ai composé bien facilement, c'est extraordinaire ; je croyais que je ne pouvais plus faire de vers. »
- Translation: "You who know my extreme littleness, you do not fear to lower yourself to me!" ; "I composed it very easily, it is extraordinary; I thought I could no longer make verses."
- New to English readers: partial (the poem is in Taylor 1912; the circumstance is new)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The original is lost; the text is known from a facsimile of the Process (session 19, transcribed by the Benedictines of Bayeux), per the Carmel page.
### F-OW-582 — "Prayer to obtain humility" (Pri 20), 16 July 1897, copied for Sr Marthe
- Claim: Pri 20 is dated on the page "16 juillet 1897". It is a prayer beginning "O Jesus, when you were a Traveller on earth you said: Learn from me that I am meek and humble of heart." She asks to submit her will to her sisters "without asking whether they have the right to command me", as Jesus obeyed even his executioners; she sees his humility in the Host, obedient to all his priests, "whether they love you or are lukewarm or cold". She asks "that my sisters always put me in the last place and that I be truly convinced this place is mine", and asks Jesus "to send me a humiliation each time I try to raise myself above others". She notes the daily failure: "each morning I take the resolution... and in the evening I see I have committed many faults of pride; I am tempted to be discouraged, but... discouragement is also pride." She repeats: "O Jesus, meek and humble of heart, make my heart like yours." LT 256 (16 Jul 1897) tells Sr Marthe she will give her "the prayer on humility, not quite finished being copied".
- When / where: 16 Jul 1897, infirmary
- People: Sr Marthe de Jésus (for whom she was copying it); Thérèse
- Source: Pri 20, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/jesus/ ; LT 256, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-256-a-soeur-marthe-de-jesus-16-juillet-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « je veux, afin de répondre à votre amour, désirer que mes soeurs me mettent toujours à la dernière place et bien me persuader que cette place est la mienne » ; « le découragement est aussi de l'orgueil »
- Translation: "To answer your love I want to desire that my sisters always put me in the last place and to be truly persuaded that this place is mine." ; "Discouragement is also pride."
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: It is one of the last long texts she wrote (the same week as the trembling-hand letters). The dedication to Sr Marthe is our link from LT 256 and the same date; the prayer page itself names no recipient.
### F-OW-583 — Her last written text (Pri 21), 8 September 1897: "If I were Queen of Heaven"
- Claim: The Carmel editors' note on Pri 21 says: "Last text in Thérèse's hand." The text, dated 8 Sep 1897 (the feast of the Nativity of Mary, and the seventh anniversary of her profession): "O Mary, if I were the Queen of Heaven and you were Thérèse, I would want to be Thérèse so that you might be the Queen of Heaven!" On the back is a picture of Our Lady of Victories, on which Thérèse had stuck the little saxifrage flower her father gave her when she asked to enter Carmel (the editors refer to Ms A 50v).
- When / where: 8 Sep 1897, infirmary of the Carmel of Lisieux (22 days before her death)
- People: the Virgin Mary (addressed); Louis Martin (the flower's giver, years earlier)
- Source: Pri 21, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/si-jetais-la-reine-du-ciel/
- Source type: own-writing (the note on "last text" and the flower: Carmel editors, facts only)
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription and note: unknown
- Original words: « O Marie, si j'étais la Reine du Ciel et que vous soyez Thérèse, je voudrais être Thérèse afin que vous soyez la Reine du Ciel !.............. » ; « [Dernier texte de la main de Thérèse. Le verso est une image de Notre Dame des Victoires sur laquelle Thérèse a collé la petite fleur de saxifrage] » (editors)
- Translation: "O Mary, if I were the Queen of Heaven and you were Thérèse, I would want to be Thérèse so that you might be the Queen of Heaven!" ; "Last text in Thérèse's hand. The back is a picture of Our Lady of Victories, on which Thérèse stuck the little saxifrage flower."
- Other sources: Ms A tells of the little white flower her father gave her when she asked to enter Carmel (Pentecost 1887, 29 May by the calendar); she put it in her Imitation of Christ at the chapter "that Jesus must be loved above all things", and the stem broke near the root (Ms A, _work/msA.f.txt line 448).
- New to English readers: yes for the flower on the image and the "last text" status; partial for the words (they are also in the Carnet jaune and Novissima Verba)
- Confidence: high for the text and date; the "last text" claim is the editors'.
- Notes: The editors' note says the flower was moved from the Imitation to this picture. Compare the card on her last letter LT 266 (25 Aug 1897). The picture is Our Lady of Victories (a Paris shrine; other cards: a novena for her cure in 1883 and again in June 1897).
